# Decision Log — Syngraphē

Append-only log of the product's design decisions. New rows go at the top of the table. A decision is changed by a new row that supersedes it, never by editing the old one. Rows decided on a branch built beside another carry that branch's epic number — `DL-10-001` — and keep it after the merge; the rest follow one sequence.

| ID | Date | Question | Decision | Rationale | Impact | Status |
|----|-----------|----------|----------|-----------|--------|--------|
| DL-23-002 | 2026-11-11 | (Refines DL-0484.) The edit tab's budget test wrote its forty-page draft straight into the database, so no capture ever ran and DL-23-001's stored answers were never made: every opening it timed took the path a person meets only when those answers are stale. How does the test make its draft? | **Through the product's own capture: an editor is opened on the new draft, the corpus captured and the worker's job that capture queues run, before the clock starts.** The corpus, the twenty openings, the 95th percentile and the 2 s budget are DL-0484's, unchanged. Tests of their own cover the stale path's answers. The other paths that write the draft — the parts, an image, a declaration, an undo — are followed by a capture of the text they changed or by the tab drawn again, which stores the answers itself, so the stale path stays rare. | A budget measures the wait a person meets. A person's draft reaches the server through capture, and a draft written behind the product's back is a state no person reaches that way. Alternatives: the stale path's 95th percentile also printed on each run without an assertion (declined: about 25 s more per CI run, for a path that a draft of one changed sentence costs about 160 ms and an unchanged one nothing); the test left as it was (declined: it would time a path the product no longer takes after an edit). | `tests/editing/browser/test_edit_tab_budget.py`; DL-23-001 | active |
| DL-23-001 | 2026-11-11 | (Refines DL-0484.) Opening the edit tab runs the normalizer over the whole draft, to learn whether the draft holds edits (DL-0510) and which declarations *Mark* offers (DL-0073). Even once that pass is shared between the two answers, it is about 300 ms of the forty-page opening. How does the opening get both answers without it? | **The draft stores both answers, with a key over everything they are read from, and the opening reads them.** When a capture writes text, it asks the worker, once it has released the writer lock, to compute both answers and store them with a key: a hash of the draft's content and base, the head commit, the document's sentence lists, the draft's staged files, the normalizer's ruleset, the whitelist specification and the version of the answers' reading. The worker skips a capture that a newer one has followed, and an answer is kept only on the draft as it was read. The edit tab uses the stored answers while the key matches. When it does not, the tab runs the normalizer and stores the result. Nothing the person sees changes: the paired acts, *My edits* and the picker behave as before. | The opening is a wait an editor meets each time they begin. A capture runs on a pause in typing; the worker's pass runs beside it, so nobody waits on it and no web request carries it. A key over every input makes a stored answer correct by construction: a path that writes the draft and does not refresh the answers costs one slower opening, never a wrong one. Alternatives: a flag set whenever a capture writes text (declined: it counts text typed and then undone back to the original as an edit, and it drifts on any path that forgets to set it); the picker reading the declarations when it first opens (declined: a wait that a stored answer avoids); the pass in the capture's own request after its lock (declined: *Finish editing* waits on a capture, and each pass would hold a web thread); the pass left on the opening (declined: the CI runner reads the forty-page opening at about 1.7 s against its 2 s budget). | `editing.Draft` gains one field (a migration); ASR-21 | active |
| DL-0543 | 2026-11-11 | (Refines DL-0311.) Before its first upload to an S3 store, the bundle tool asks the store to create the bucket, which a key confined to the bucket's objects is refused, so no bundle leaves the host. May the platform's key create a bucket? | **No: the platform never creates or checks the bucket.** The bucket is made by the operator before `.env` names it, with the versioning and lifecycle DL-0298 requires; the platform's key needs only to list the bucket and to read, write and delete its objects, and the bundle tool writes into the bucket as it finds it. | The right to create a bucket reaches beyond the one store the host may write to, and a bucket the platform created would carry none of the controls DL-0298 relies on, which only its creator sets. Alternative: grant the key bucket creation, so the tool's check passes (declined: a right the platform never needs, held on a host DL-0298 assumes can be taken). | `documents/backup.py` (the S3 remote); `runbook.md § Backup` (*The store*); DL-0298 | active |

(24) DL-0192 in part — by DL-0194; (25) DL-0194 in part — by DL-0197; (26) DL-0198 in part — by DL-0267; (27) DL-0210 in part — by DL-0231; (28) DL-0232 in part — the notice's words, by DL-11-005; (29) DL-0251 in part — by DL-0534; (30) DL-0291 in part — by DL-0376; (31) DL-0306 in part — the interim before the notifying step was built; (32) DL-0334 in part — the interim before the reading view, built as DL-0356 says; (33) DL-0343 in part — the interim before the reset of ST-0055, now built; The later rows stand as written; this row names each supersession once, and each row above names this one in its status. | A status names what replaced its row, so that a reader can follow the chain; a later row's Question is never edited, so this row states each claim that row did not make. Alternatives: change the old rows' status alone (declined: each would name a row that does not claim it); leave them as they are (declined: they read as current while the product does otherwise). | `docs/design-record/decision-log.md` — the status of each row named above | active |
| DL-0541 | 2026-11-04 | (Supersedes DL-0523 and DL-0525 in part.) The menu row ends in the person's display name, the link to their account page; a long name takes much of the row. Does the name stay there? | **The row ends in a person icon, the link to the account page; the name is its tooltip and accessible label, and the account page shows it.** The address stands in for the name while the person has none, as before. | The row is the one place every page shares, and a name of any length crowds it; an icon keeps it short. The name stays one hover away and is read aloud by a screen reader, so a person holding several accounts can still tell which one is signed in. | the shell (`base.html`'s account link, `shell.css`); identity (the account page names the person); `docs/interface/pages.md` (Shell; the account page); the help (people, in both languages); the tests that read the name in the row | active |
| DL-0540 | 2026-11-04 | (Refines DL-0526.) With the outline and the panel collapsed, the text's column widens, but the text keeps its 768 px measure and stays at the column's left, a wide empty band beside it. Where does the text stand? | **The text keeps its measure and is centred in its column.** Collapsing a pane gives the text room on both sides, never a longer line. | A line of 768 px is the measure the reading view was set to; a longer one is harder to read. Centred, the freed width reads as margin rather than as a missing column. | rendering (`document.css`, the text's box in the panes' grid); `docs/interface/pages.md` (the panes) | active |
| DL-0539 | 2026-11-04 | (Refines DL-0523.) A person edits their own display name on the account page. Once set, may they clear it and be named by their address again? | **A display name, once set, stays: it can be changed, never cleared.** The account page's field is required; a person without one is named by their address until they set one. | Colleagues come to know a person by the name they chose; a name that can disappear makes the same person look like someone else on the next page. Changing it keeps that continuity, clearing it would break it. | identity (the account page's field); `docs/interface/pages.md` (the account page); the help (people) | active |
| DL-0538 | 2026-11-03 | (Refines DL-0537; amends ASR-17 and T-14 in part.) Refusing an invitation that would bring in an existing person whose display name another member uses makes the invite page answer differently for an address known elsewhere, when its name collides: a tenant administrator who sets their own name, or knows a member's, can learn that an address belongs to a person of that name elsewhere. Does the refusal stay? | **The refusal stays, and the disclosure is accepted**: ASR-17 and T-14 name it as a residual — the invite page tells a known address from an unknown one only when the person's display name is already used in the organisation, and only to its tenant administrators. Every other answer of the invitation stays alike. Until people can tell two members of one name apart by another means, an identical name is refused wherever a person names themselves or joins (DL-0523, DL-0537); a page of a person's own that others open, the display name linking to it, is backlog B-060. Alternative: the invitation never checks the name, the clash standing until a rename (declined). | The owner's choice: two members of one name on the organisation's pages confuse every reader, while the disclosure needs a name match and reaches tenant administrators alone. | `docs/asr.md` ASR-17; `docs/threat-model.md` T-14; `docs/backlog.md` B-060; `identity/invitations.py` (as built) | active |
| DL-0537 | 2026-11-03 | (Refines DL-0523.) An invitation carries no name but can bring in an existing person who has one, as `create_member` can for an existing address, and that name may be taken in the organisation they join. Is joining refused, and what does a refused name say? | **Joining is refused** — the invitation and `create_member` alike — while the person's display name is used by another active or blocked member of the organisation they would join, saying that this person's display name is already used by a member of the organisation, never the name itself; the person changes it on their account page first. **A refused name names no organisation**: *Another member already uses this name.* · *Це ім’я вже використовує інший учасник.* The field is *Display name* · *Ім’я для показу*, with *Apply*. Alternative: joining allowed, the clash standing until one of the two renames (declined). | The owner's choice: uniqueness holds from the moment a person joins, and a refusal tells nobody which organisations, or whose name, it collided with. | `identity/names.py`; the account page; `identity/invitations.py` and the invitation page; `create_member`; the `uk` catalogue; `docs/user-stories.md` ST-0078 | active |
| DL-0536 | 2026-11-03 | (Refines DL-0526; supersedes DL-0195 and DL-0356 in part.) DL-0526 has the right panel's sections collapse one by one, each named while collapsed, but the panel's icon row shows one section at a time — *Parts* or *Generated views*. What is a section, and does the icon row stay? | **The panel's sections stand one under another in its column, each under its name, which collapses and opens it**; a collapsed section keeps its name. With every section collapsed the column narrows to its strip, each section's name drawn vertically in it, and a name opens the panel on that section. The panel's own control collapses the whole column as before, and the person's collapse is kept. **The icon row goes.** A panel of one section — the review page's versions, the draft's parts panel — collapses whole, its name in the strip. Comments, when they come, are a section (B-013). Alternatives: the icon row kept, the panel collapsing whole to a strip of names (declined); the icon row kept, the blocks inside a section collapsing to their headings (declined). | The owner's choice: what is collapsed stays named, and a reader opens what they need beside what is already open. | `rendering/panes_panel.html`, `rendering/static/rendering/panel.js`, `document.css`; `docs/interface/pages.md § Document page` (the right panel); the panel's browser tests | active |
| DL-0535 | 2026-11-03 | (Refines DL-0532.) DL-0532 has lists, History and notifications show a summary's first line, but no notification draws a summary and DL-0205 keeps it out of a notification's body. Does a notification show one, and how long may the whole summary be? | **No notification shows a summary**, as built: the first line is drawn in the run lists and in History's rows, the whole on the version's page and in the review page's panel. **The whole summary is at most 5,000 characters**, its first line keeping its own limit of 500. Alternative: the notifications page drawing the first line, read from the version when drawn (declined). | The owner's choice: DL-0205's rule stands, and a summary is read where its version is. | `editing.summary`; `docs/interface/pages.md` (the send-for-review screen, Notifications); `docs/data-model.md` (`VERSION.summary`) | active |
| DL-0534 | 2026-11-03 | (Refines DL-0524 and DL-0251.) *Accept*, *freeze* and *reserve for editing* include *edit* (ADR-0016). DL-0524 gives a holder of *edit* History from the latest edition up, while DL-0251 gives a holder of *accept* without a history right the pending run alone, so a reviewer would see less of History than an editor. Which rule holds for a holder of *accept*? | **Every holder of *edit*, however they hold it — through *accept*, *freeze* or *reserve for editing* included — reads History from the latest canonical edition up**, with no edition yet from the document's first version; the ceiling every historical page, comparison and export asks reaches the same versions. A history right widens it as built: *view all history* every version, *view previous canonical versions* the editions besides. DL-0251's *the pending run alone* for *accept* is subsumed, the run lying above the edition. Alternative: *accept* kept at the run alone (declined: the reviewer would see less than the editors whose work they accept). | The owner's choice. A right that includes another never reaches less than it. | `review.history` (`HISTORY_RIGHTS`, `entries`, `ceiling`); `docs/interface/pages.md § History and Info`; the help's *History* page; the history tests | active |
| DL-0533 | 2026-11-03 | (Refines DL-0522.) *More* (⋯) holds the administrator's acts on a document. Is it drawn to a platform administrator who does not administer the organisation? | **Yes.** A platform administrator who has chosen an organisation in the switcher acts there as its administrator: *More* and its acts are drawn to them as to the organisation's own administrators, and the pages that describe them say *tenant or platform administrator*. | The platform administrator is the one who acts when an organisation's own administrator cannot; drawing the acts and refusing them would be a page that lies. | `docs/interface/pages.md § Document page` (the *More* row and *Groups*); no code change — the build already does this (`administers(request)`) | active |
| DL-0532 | 2026-11-03 | (Refines DL-0261 and DL-0513.) The finishing screen puts *Send for review* and the change summary below the text, and the summary is one line of at most 500 characters because it is the commit's first line, read back from it. Where do they stand, and how long may a summary be? | **The summary and the screen's buttons stand above the text**, the changes below them. **The summary is plain text of several lines**, written in a field the editor's width and about ten lines high: its first line is the commit's subject, the rest the commit's body. Lists, History and notifications show its first line; the version's page and the review page's panel show it whole. The first line keeps today's rules — required, at most 500 characters, not beginning like a trailer — and no line of the body may read as a trailer. | A reviewer reads why before what; a change worth reviewing often needs more than a line to explain. The commit keeps carrying the whole summary, so the record stays the repository's. | `editing.summary` (the rules by line), the finishing screen and its form, the commit's message and its reading back into `VERSION.summary`, the review panel and the version's page; `docs/interface/pages.md` (the send-for-review screen); `docs/data-model.md` (`VERSION.summary`); the help; tests | partly superseded — DL-0542 |
| DL-0531 | 2026-11-03 | The editor's toolbar starts with formatting and keeps undo and redo near its end, and offers no way to strip formatting from a passage. What comes first, and what does stripping remove? | **Undo and redo are the first two controls, *Clear formatting* the third**, the rest as now. *Clear formatting* acts on the selection, or on the line the cursor stands in when nothing is selected: it removes bold, italic, underline and links, lifts the lines out of lists, quotes and the note blocks, and turns headings into paragraphs — leaving plain paragraphs of the same words. | Undo and redo are the controls used most and searched for first; text pasted or rearranged often needs its formatting removed in one act. | the editor's toolbar and a new command in the bundle (`src/editing/js/`); the editor's help; the toolbar's browser tests | active |

| DL-0522 | 2026-11-02 | (Refines DL-0518.) The path's design leaves open its addresses, what a reader of pending work without *accept* opens, where the administrator's acts and the undo controls go, what the appendix page shows, how confirmations are drawn and where a kept draft is offered. How? | **Every point and arc has a page and an address of its own, today's kept** — a version's page, the comparison, `edit/`, `pending/` as the review page, `history/`, `info/` — so links in notifications and emails answer. Comparing two documents stands under the first document's heading and path, the heading reading `#A x → #B y`. **An arc opens the comparison of its two versions; a version's point opens that version to read**; the review page is for a holder of *accept* with a run pending. **A reader of pending work without *accept* reaches the run by its arc**, whose page draws the run's change authors in the margin, a changed image before and after side by side, and the run's part operations, read-only — the review page without its acts; `pending/` answers such a reader with it. **The administrator's acts** — *Групи*, *Архівувати* / *Відновити*, *Звільнити документ примусово*, *Завершити резервування…* — stand behind a fourth icon in the heading, *Більше* (⋯), drawn to administrators alone — the fourth for administrators; *Analysis*, when it comes, is the next (DL-0518). **The appendix page carries the document's path**: a point opens the appendix at that version, editing opens the appendix's editor. **The undo controls move from History to the review page**; History lists, opens and compares. **Accept, return, finish and end a reservation stay pages of their own** inside the frame, the path above them; discarding and *make canonical* are windows (DL-0513). **A kept draft is the holder's draft point, marked *залишена***, with *Забрати документ назад* and *Відкинути цю чернетку…*, read-only once the document has moved on (DL-0225, DL-0318). Rehearsing a restore beside the running installation is how an operator proves the backup without stopping it, and the one way it can harm the installation is by writing to the store it reads; a tested file keeps that guarantee true after a later change to the base file, such as a new service that writes to the store. It names nothing about any installation. | `deploy/compose.rehearsal.yaml`; `tests/foundation/`; `runbook.md § Restore`; `technical-design.md` | active |
| DL-0495 | 2026-10-28 | (Refines DL-0298.) A versioned store keeps every deleted file; a store protected by its own snapshots keeps what each snapshot held. May production's backup store be an SFTP store whose host takes snapshots, and what does it then guarantee? | **Yes: an SFTP store whose own host takes a snapshot of the installation's path daily and keeps each for thirty days, the snapshots beyond the reach of the account the platform signs in with — it can neither change nor remove one.** A deletion from the platform's host, retention's or an intruder's, then destroys at most what was written since the last snapshot — up to a day of the archive and the bundles — and hides the rest for thirty days. Alternatives: hourly snapshots, at most an hour lost (declined); a versioned S3 bucket, nothing lost (declined). | The owner's choice, accepting up to a day of the backup's history as the bound against an intruder on the host. Recovery from the store being intact is unchanged: the bound applies only when the store has been emptied from the host, and then the database restores to the newest snapshot that holds a clean marker. | `threat-model.md` T-20; `runbook.md § Backup`; the production backup store's choice | active |
| DL-0494 | 2026-10-28 | (Applies DL-0374.) Invitations and password reset reach people by email. Does the platform launch before it has a sending address? | **No. The platform launches with its sending address set and one real email confirmed sent and received**, so the first real users are invited and can reset a password from the first day. Alternatives: launch with mail off, people added by the operator's command and no password reset (declined); deploy and verify with mail off, inviting people only once mail works (declined). | The owner's choice. Without a sender every person enters by command with a password the operator hands over, and a forgotten password has no remedy but the operator; the launch is the point at which real people arrive. | `runbook.md § Mail`; the launch review's readiness items | active |
| DL-0493 | 2026-10-28 | What may a page load and run, and where is that policy set? | **Every response carries a `Content-Security-Policy` set by the application, on every installation: the platform's own scripts, styles, images and fonts only, nothing inline but style attributes.** Scripts from the platform alone and never evaluated; style sheets from the platform alone, with `style` attributes allowed (`style-src-attr 'unsafe-inline'`), since the pages set their panes' widths, a folder's depth and a table cell's alignment that way; no plugin, no frame of the platform inside another site, forms posted to the platform only. Alternatives: the header set by the production front alone (declined: the development stack and the browser tests would never run under it, and a page broken by the policy would first be seen on production); every inline style attribute replaced, leaving no inline exception (declined at the release's end: eight templates and the editor's alignment change, for a residual risk that an attribute alone cannot carry a selector). | The owner's choice. A policy in the application is tested wherever the application runs, so the editor and every page are exercised under it before a deploy; the one exception is the narrowest that the pages as built need. | a middleware in `src/syngraphe/`; `threat-model.md` (the attribute exception); `technical-design.md`; the browser tests run under it | active |
| DL-0492 | 2026-10-28 | (Restates DL-0345's rule, superseded with the preview by DL-0364.) How does a release reach production, and what may run there? | **Production runs an annotated tag only, on a commit whose CI run is green with every step run: `v0.1.0-rc.N` for each candidate deployed, `v0.1.0` on the commit the launch review closes on.** The tag reaches the host as a `git archive` of it, uploaded and extracted into a folder of its own beside the running release's, never over it; its images are built on the production host from that tree and tagged with the release, so the previous release's images stay for a rollback. The host holds no credential for the repository. If the host cannot reach a source the image build needs, the images are built for the host's architecture elsewhere and pulled from a private registry instead. Alternatives: a pull of the tag over SSH with a read-only deploy key (declined); images built elsewhere by default (declined). | The owner's choice. The archive needs no credential and no delivery machinery on a host the platform does not own; the tag ties what runs to a commit CI proved; building on the host sidesteps the difference between the development machine's architecture and production's. | `runbook.md § Deploy` (*Delivery*); the deployment architecture | active |
| DL-0491 | 2026-10-28 | (Applies DL-0040 to the historical page.) A past version's page now names who wrote each line. Alternative: authors always, the toggle removed (declined: the text could no longer be read without attribution, which DL-0040 keeps, and each first view of a past version would start a background job to compute its authors). | DL-0040 makes attribution free and visible to every reader, and readable without it; the historical page follows the same rule as the pending tab rather than a second one. | the historical page's toggle; the reader's preference | active |
| DL-0490 | 2026-10-19 | (Extends DL-0483 and DL-0486.) Alternatives: removal, as in a document's text (declined: a short field is typed, and a refusal names what is wrong); leave them (declined: a reviewer can approve a title whose visible form differs from what is stored). | One rule for every short field a person types: what a reviewer reads in a title is what is stored and printed. | `documents.bundle.valid_title` and its callers; `documents.entities` (an entity's label); their tests; `docs/backlog.md` B-057 | active |

The glossary's *show author* becomes *edit author*, fixed. Alternative: *change author* (declined: the product calls a change an edit — *Pending edits*, *My edits*). | A document has a creator; a line has whoever last edited it, and the label must not confuse the two. | `review/templates/review/pending.html`; `rendering/templates/rendering/historical.html`; the `uk` catalogue; `editing/help/{en,uk}/review.md`; `docs/glossary.md`; `docs/interface/pages.md` | active |
| DL-0488 | 2026-10-19 | (Applies ST-0064.) The historical page offers *compare with another document* beside *compare with*: a field for a document's number. | DL-0487 | 2026-10-19 | (Refines DL-0486.) Which characters does a document's text lose or refuse, and what of a line stored before the rule, which ASR-20 keeps byte for byte while the normaliser canonicalises the whole submitted text? | **By category, as DL-0483 and DL-0485 decide for the short fields: every format character (Unicode category Cf) is removed, but the zero-width non-joiner and joiner (U+200C, U+200D), which are kept, and the marks that reorder text (U+202A to U+202E, U+2066 to U+2069), which are refused, each named by its line.** U+FEFF, refused until now, is removed with its kin; U+2028 and U+2029 stay refused. **Removal reaches only the lines a person changes or pastes**: a line stored before keeps its bytes until it is edited (ASR-20), and a stored reordering mark makes the text refused when the editor opens it, as any refused construct is, the line named. Alternatives: only the characters DL-0486 names removed, every other format character refused (declined); removal on every submit (declined: it rewrites stored lines). | A rule by category holds as Unicode grows, as DL-0485 argued; ASR-20 forbids re-normalising a stored line, and the editor already shows a text it cannot accept in its source view with the line named. | `editing/markdown/rules.py` and `editing/js/rules.js` (`CONTROL`); the normaliser; the paste conversions; the whitelist specification's conformance cases; the `uk` catalogue; `docs/technical-design.md` § The constrained Markdown subset | active |
| DL-0486 | 2026-10-19 | (Extends DL-0483 and DL-0485 from two short fields to a document's text.) The editor's rules refuse control characters, U+2028, U+2029 and U+FEFF, but admit zero-width spaces, soft hyphens, word joiners and the marks that reorder text, so a clause can display otherwise than it is stored and a reviewer accept wording they did not see. What does a document's text admit? | **What is read and what is stored are the same.** In a document's text — the body and every appendix — the invisible characters that change nothing a reader sees are **removed at paste and at normalisation**: the zero-width space, the soft hyphen, the word joiner, the left-to-right and right-to-left marks and their kin; **the zero-width joiner and non-joiner (U+200D, U+200C) are kept**, since some scripts need them. **The marks that reorder text are refused**, each named by its line: the bidirectional embeddings and overrides (U+202A to U+202E) and isolates (U+2066 to U+2069). Both rule sets carry it (ADR-0010), with the normaliser and the paste conversions. Alternatives: refuse every invisible character (declined: text pasted from a word processor carries soft hyphens and direction marks harmlessly, and would be refused); leave them (declined: a reviewer can accept wording they did not see). | Review means approving the words as they are; a character that hides or reorders what is shown breaks that guarantee, and removing the harmless ones keeps ordinary pastes working. | `editing/markdown/rules.py` and `editing/js/rules.js` (`CONTROL`); the normaliser; the paste conversions; `docs/threat-model.md` T-24; `docs/technical-design.md` § The constrained Markdown subset | active |
| DL-0485 | 2026-10-19 | (Refines DL-0483.) The rule for a reason and for the title-page text refuses every invisible format character (Unicode category Cf) but the two joiners. | DL-11-001 | 2026-10-16 | (Applies DL-0175 and ADR-0006.) An image upload reaches the server after the person's hold was released — forced, for inactivity, or by a block. | DL-10-003 | 2026-10-16 | (Applies DL-0197.) Search finds a document by the title of an appendix of the version its reader reads. An appendix title becomes a candidate only at one of those versions, so no row beyond them reaches the application and a draft never accepted is never a candidate. Residual, accepted: the cost can still tell of an appendix title of earlier accepted or canonical text the reader could read when it was current, or of a version committed in the same second as the latest acceptance (threat model, accepted risks). Alternatives: the search as first built, any version a candidate, the oracle recorded as accepted (declined); a cut-off by time alone (declined: it let a never-accepted draft below it through, and fetched the rows beyond it before discarding them); each acceptance's content version stored on its row for an exact reach (declined: a migration and a data-model change for a narrow residual). | A title a reader may not see stays unseen whichever surface would carry it, its timing included; the database already holds the editions, the acceptances and their predecessors and the commit times the reach needs. | `documents/search.py` (`find`, `appendix_candidates`, `_readable_versions`); `docs/threat-model.md` (accepted risks) | active |

| DL-0431 | 2026-10-15 | When does CI run once feature branches exist? | **On every push to every branch, and so on the merge commit on `main`.** A branch is tested at each push, before and after its pull request is opened; the pull request is merged with a merge commit once its head is green; `main` is tested again on the merge. | DL-0430 | 2026-10-15 | The test databases are created on the stack's PostgreSQL, which archives every WAL segment to the off-host backup store, so every test run's log is uploaded with the stack's own. CI's service gets the same three settings. The suite refuses a server whose `archive_mode` is not off. The stack's `db` keeps archiving in every shape; production reads only `compose.yaml` and `deploy/compose.prod.yaml`, and a test asserts that its database archives and that it has no `test-db`. | A test run's log is not data to back up: it costs the backup store's bandwidth and space and puts traffic into the history a restore replays. A test server needs nothing to survive a crash, so durability is turned off there; measured, archiving cost about 14 s of a four-minute run and durability about 28 s more. Turning archiving off on the stack's own `db`, even in the developer override, would leave its backup unexercised on a workstation. | `deploy/compose.dev.yaml`; `.env.example`; `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; `tests/conftest.py`; `tests/foundation/test_production_override.py`; root `CLAUDE.md § Commands`; `docs/test-strategy.md` | active |

| DL-0428 | 2026-10-14 | The full check runs the suite serially and takes over half an hour, too long to run after every unit of work. How does the check run the tests? | **In parallel workers, with the timing budgets apart.** `pytest-xdist` joins the development dependencies. The check's test step is `uv run pytest -q -n auto -m "not budget" && uv run pytest -q -m budget`: every test but the timing budgets on one worker per core, then the budgets, marked `budget`, serially; CI runs the second as a step of its own. `-n` is not in `addopts`, so a single file still runs in one process. Each worker has a test database of its own, and every run a database name of its own, so two runs on one machine never share one. | A timing budget measures the machine as much as the code: beside other workers it fails and alone it passes, so it is judged alone. Workers need nothing shared between them that a test writes — a database, a repository root, a lock file — and the suite's fixtures give each worker its own. The same line for a developer and for CI keeps one check. | Root `CLAUDE.md § Commands`; `docs/test-strategy.md § What CI runs`; `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; `pyproject.toml` (the dependency, the `budget` marker); `tests/conftest.py` | active |

| DL-0414 | 2026-10-14 | (Refines DL-0318, DL-0335 and DL-0256.) A finish can stop after its commit, before its hold is released; if the hold is then forced free, ended by an administrator, released for inactivity or ended by a block before the reconciler runs, the draft is kept as preserved although the commit already holds it. The reconciler repairs only open holds, so the reviewers are never told, and the holder returns to a read-only view with nothing in it. What does a release do then? | **A release that is not the holder's own first finishes a stopped finish.** Under the document's locks, before releasing `forced`, `inactivity` or `blocked`, `holds.release` settles the record first, as every writer does, then asks the finish's own question of the hold: a content commit of the holder above the draft's base with nothing but acceptances after it, the draft holding nothing more. Where the answer is yes, the hold is released `finished`, the reviewers are told the version awaits them and the draft is deleted, as the reconciler would have done; nothing is preserved and the act reports the document free. Otherwise the release proceeds as asked. Alternatives: the reconciler extended to released drafts (declined — up to an hour in which the holder sees an empty comparison and the reviewers wait untold); both (declined — the release settles every case it meets, so the repair would find nothing). | Every writer settles what is committed before acting on it; a release is a writer of the hold, and a draft whose text is already a version is not work at risk. | `editing/holds.py` (`release`); `identity/blocking.py` (`end_holds` inherits it); `data-model.md` (`HOLD.release_kind`); `technical-design.md`; the force release, the end of a reservation and the inactivity release | active |

| DL-0384 | 2026-10-13 | (Refines DL-0202 and DL-0372.) The key that encrypts the sending passwords is needed by the processes that save a sender and send mail, and by no other; every container runs the same settings, and a key the settings require at start stops the renderer, which is never given it. Where is the key read? | **At the point of use, not at start.** `MAIL_PASSWORD_KEY` and the optional `MAIL_PASSWORD_KEY_PREVIOUS` are read from the environment when a password is encrypted or decrypted, through the configuration reader, and a missing key is reported by name with every other missing key at that moment, as the backup's own secrets are. Only `web` and `worker` are given them; the stack starts without them, and a sender cannot be saved, or a mail sent, until the key is set. Alternatives: required at start and given to the renderer too (declined: the sandboxed renderer would hold the key to every sending password); a per-service list of required keys at start (declined: machinery for one key). | A secret is held only by the processes that use it; the renderer, which reads untrusted document content, holds none. | `technical-design.md` (notifications: the keys); `runbook.md` (mail); `.env.example`; `compose.yaml` (`web` and `worker`) | active |

Alternative: one rule per host — the domain's organisation, else the platform's — without reading the person's memberships (declined). | A person's mail should leave under the name of the organisation they work in where that is unambiguous, and under the platform's where it is not or where the platform itself is addressed. | `technical-design.md` (identity: the reset); the reset's queueing step; `data-model.md` (the placement prose) | active |

Where do they live? | **A table of their own in the shared schema, `PLATFORM_NOTIFICATION`** — the person, the event, the facts, when created, read and emailed, no document columns — in a shared app; the organisation's `NOTIFICATION` table stays as it is, on the organisation list alone. **The mail tables** — the sender and the outbox — keep ADR-0017's both-schema placement in an app of their own, the data model's *mail app*. It is *discard my edits* applied to a preserved draft, not a new act. Alternatives: *start afresh*, taking a new hold and discarding the old draft in one act (declined); both controls (declined). | One act for throwing a draft away, wherever the draft is met; the holder reads the comparison first and decides. | `user-stories.md` ST-0070; `pages.md § The edit tab` (the read-only view's row); the edit tab; `document-lifecycle.md` | active |
| DL-0380 | 2026-10-13 | (Refines ADR-0015 and DL-0243.) The sentences a later version changed, which an undo leaves for the reviewer's hand, are *listed at the top of the draft*. | DL-0375 | 2026-10-13 | (Closes the risk Epic 5 carried.) Every act — a commit, an acceptance, a freeze, an approval, taking a hold — re-reads the person's rights under the document's lock before it writes; a revocation is a transaction of its own, not ordered by that lock. A right revoked after that read and before the act's commit is not seen for that one act. Is the window accepted? | **Yes, accepted**: the window is one act wide, named in the threat model among the accepted risks, with a test that shows it. Alternative: serialising every revocation against the acts by locking each linked document's row inside a group change (declined — a group may be linked to the whole corpus). | The next act by the same person is refused; what the window admits is one act already under way, which the record attributes as any other. | `threat-model.md § Accepted risks`; `access/models.py` unchanged; a test in `tests/access/` | active |
| DL-0374 | 2026-10-13 | (Applies ASR-17 and DL-0107.) An invited person who is new to the platform and one registered in another organisation must look identical to the inviting administrator. An administrator who wonders invites again, which sends a fresh link. Alternative: an acceptance mark on the list (declined — it is the oracle ASR-17 forbids). | The membership's `granted_at` is what an administrator sees (ASR-17); creating both rows in the same act makes the two cases one row shape, and nothing later can tell them apart. | `data-model.md` (the prose on invitations); the users page; `threat-model.md` T-14 | active |

How long does each live, and where is a reset recorded? | **An invitation lives seven days; a reset link one hour.** A reset is recorded in a table of its own, **`PASSWORD_RESET`** — the person, the token's hash, its expiry and when it was used — in the invitation's shape. Alternatives: other spans (none proposed); reusing `INVITATION` with a `kind` column (declined). | An invitation waits for a person to find time; a reset is asked for and used at once, and a short life bounds a stolen link. | DL-0361 | 2026-10-06 | (Refines ST-0032.) A document with a long run of versions is read one change at a time. It is offered in the thinnest form of the parts panel — attachments only, title and size, *download* the one action; the full panel of ST-0061 to ST-0063 grows from the same route. | The route and the storage are the ones the parts epic would have built; laying them now means that epic adds controls and review, not a second path. The version's kind follows ADR-0014: `reviewer_edit` when its author holds *accept*. What does the lock cover? | **Writing the bundle file, and nothing after it.** The run takes the lock, writes the bundle, releases the lock, then verifies the bundle and copies it; the refs recorded beside the repository are rewritten only once the copy is confirmed. To what point is the database restored? | **The earliest of three: the newest clean marker's start, the last moment known to be good, and the end of the archive's continuous range after the base backup restored** — a gap left by an outage of the store (DL-0297) ends that range, and `pgbackrest verify` reports it. When the end of the range is the earliest, the database is restored to it rather than to a time. The restore script prints all three and which one governs before it writes anything. Alternative: keep the target and document the error in the runbook (agent, declined). | Each bounds the point from above for its own reason — the repositories, the damage, what was archived — so the point is their minimum, and it is still never later than the repositories. | ADR-0019; `runbook.md § Restore` case A step 4; the database restore script | partly superseded — DL-0314 (the whole-host restore's target) |

How does the worker learn it? | **The `backup` service tells it.** After `pgbackrest backup` succeeds, the service writes the backup's label and stop time to a file on a small volume the worker can read; the first run that finds a label newer than its last full bundles writes new ones. The store's credentials and both keys are on the host, and retention deletes from the host, so whoever takes the host — the loss the off-host copy exists for — can remove every backup. Encryption answers who can read the store, not who can empty it. Who may destroy what is in the backup store? | **In production, nothing on the host, for thirty days.** The production backup store accepts the host's writes and deletions without destroying what was written: a versioned object store whose credentials on the host cannot delete a version, the store's own lifecycle removing a superseded or deleted version after thirty days, or an equivalent the store enforces. A store that cannot do this is not a production backup store. A deletion from the host — retention's or an intruder's — then hides a file for thirty days rather than destroying it. **The development store is exempt**, as an accepted risk: it holds invented content only. Alternatives: an accepted risk in production too, named in the threat model (agent, declined); credentials that can add but not delete on an SFTP store, which break pgBackRest's own expiry and the bundle retention and move deletion to a job on the store's host (agent, declined). | The host holds both the right to write and the right to delete, and the off-host copy exists for the day the host cannot be trusted. | DL-0297 | 2026-09-28 | When the backup store is unreachable, PostgreSQL keeps every segment of the write-ahead log it could not archive; on a host whose data disk is shared with other workloads, a long outage fills the disk and stops them all. Does the archive give way, and when? | **At a size the installation sets, the archive gives way before the disk does.** pgBackRest archives asynchronously through its spool, and `archive-push-queue-max` caps what waits: past it, pgBackRest acknowledges segments without shipping them, so the live database loses nothing and the disk stops growing, but the archive has a gap that point-in-time recovery cannot cross. The operator is alerted at a lower size. Both sizes are configuration, defaulting to 2 GiB for the alert and 10 GiB for the cap. The runbook's step for a store outage: bring the store back, confirm the archive ships again, and take a full base backup at once, which starts a recoverable range after the gap; until then a restore aims no later than the end of the archive. Alternative: never drop a segment, relying on the alert and the runbook (agent, declined — a long outage would still end in a full disk). | A backup that stops the database it protects, and whatever shares its disk, turns an outage of the store into an outage of the platform. What is the interval? | **The bundle run starts every ten minutes, and ASR-25's fifteen minutes stands**: it holds while a run takes less than five. ADR-0018's review trigger becomes: a bundle run takes longer than five minutes; the nightly run of full bundles is measured against it when backup is implemented. Alternative: keep the fifteen-minute interval, state the recovery point as fifteen minutes plus one run, and alert at thirty (agent, declined). | The figure an operator is held to should be one the platform meets. | DL-0295 | 2026-09-28 | (Completes DL-0294.) How does the operator learn that the backup has stopped keeping up? | **Through two channels.** `/health/` gains a `backup` entry — the age of the newest clean marker, whether any repository is failing (never which, nor how many, since the endpoint answers anyone), the time of the last segment archived, and the size of the write-ahead log not yet archived — without changing the endpoint's status code, so a stale backup never marks `web` unhealthy. And every platform administrator receives an *Administrative* notification and an email through the outbox (DL-0203) when a repository's bundle first fails, when the newest clean marker becomes older than fifteen minutes, and when the unarchived log passes its alert threshold; each condition notifies once when it begins and once when it clears, and carries numbers and ages, never a title. Alternatives: the health endpoint alone, polled by the operator's own monitor (agent, declined); email alone (agent, declined). | A log line was the only signal, and a log is read after the loss. | DL-0294 | 2026-09-28 | (Supersedes ADR-0018 in part; refines DL-0276.) A run's completion marker is written only when every bundle of the run has verified and reached the store. One repository that fails on every run — a damaged repository, an error writing its bundle, a lock never free — would then stop every marker for every tenant, and the whole-host recovery point would stay where it was, possibly days back; and a run with nothing to bundle wrote no marker at all. What does a run record when some of its bundles fail? | **Every run writes its marker, a run with nothing to bundle included, and the marker lists each repository whose bundle failed with the time its consecutive failures began.** A writer lock not taken within a timeout counts as a failure of that repository. **A marker listing no failure is a clean marker**, and a whole-host restore aims the database at the newest clean marker's start by default, so by default the database is still never restored past any repository (DL-0276). When a newer marker with failures exists, the runbook puts the choice to the operator at the restore — the one exception to that rule, bounded to the repositories the marker names and decided by the operator, never by the platform: the clean marker, giving up both stores' changes since it began, or the newer marker, giving up the named repositories' commits since their last bundle, which the reconciler then reports as rows whose commit is missing. Alternatives: exclude a repository from the set after N consecutive failures and always restore to the newest marker (agent, declined — the platform would decide on its own to lose one document's commits, and N would need tuning); keep the all-or-nothing marker and only add an alert (agent, declined). | The all-or-nothing marker made one document's fault every tenant's recovery point. Listing the failures keeps the strict rule as the default and gives the operator, at the moment it matters, the facts to trade one named loss against everyone's. | ADR-0019; `runbook.md § Restore` case A step 4; the bundle run | active |
| DL-0293 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines DL-0289.) How does a person who has signed in before get past that? | **A device cookie.** A successful sign-in gives the browser a cookie signed with the installation's secret, carrying a hash of the address signed in with, a hash tied to the current password, and a random value, valid for ninety days; signing in again re-issues the same value, and a password change retires every cookie. An attempt for that same address presenting a valid cookie is counted against the cookie's own row alone, never the client address's or the pair's, so failures from anyone else behind the address never delay it; its own failures delay it as any row's do. A missing, altered, expired or other-address cookie leaves the attempt counted as before. | The owner's browser proves it has signed in before, which an attacker sharing the address cannot forge; a stolen cookie skips only the address's delay, never the password, and its own failures still slow it. | DL-0290 | 2026-09-28 | (Supersedes ADR-0017 in part.) Which gives way? | **Creating a tenant is the one use of the schema context manager inside a request**, confined to the service that creates it, which restores the shared schema's path on leaving. The request returns the organisation active once its schema is migrated; a failure leaves it `migrating`, resolved by no request, until `migrate_schemas --tenant N` completes it (DL-0280). Every other request sets the path only through the middleware. | The creation already runs on a platform host with no tenant resolved, so the path it sets belongs to no one else's request; one named file is a boundary a search can hold. A worker task would leave the administrator waiting on a state the page must poll for, for a migration that takes a fraction of a second. | `tenancy/services.py`; `tests/tenancy/test_path_setters.py`, which fails if any other module sets the path or uses the context manager | active |
| DL-0289 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines DL-0116.) Counting sign-in failures by the address typed, across every client, lets anyone who knows an address keep its owner out with one wrong attempt a minute — the lock DL-0116 rules out. What are failures counted against? | **The pair of the address typed and the client, and the client alone.** Each delays the next attempt 1, 2, 4 … 60 seconds after its last failure; an attempt made while either delay runs is refused and counted. A success resets the pair's count only, so signing into one's own account does not clear the client's. The pair is stored as a hash, never the address; an IPv6 client counts by its /64. | Someone else's failures never delay the owner signing in from their own client, and every source is still slowed however many addresses it tries. The address-wide count had no bound an outsider could not trigger. | `identity/throttle.py`, `SIGN_IN_ATTEMPT.key_kind`; `data-model.md` (`SIGN_IN_ATTEMPT`) | active |
| DL-0288 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines DL-0211 for V-12, now proven.) What does the suite keep asserting? | **Ten schemas under sixty seconds, and twenty schemas within 1.2 × twice the time of ten.** The one-schema time is measured and reported, never asserted on. The three-point linearity V-12 names was proven once and is recorded with its timings in V-12's status. | The twenty-against-ten ratio compares two multi-second measurements, so runner noise stays well inside its margin, and it still catches a per-schema cost that grows with the number of schemas. | `tests/tenancy/test_migrate_schemas.py`; `verification-plan.md` (V-12's status) | active |

| DL-0286 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines ADR-0007 and DL-0234.) Do the history rights — *view previous canonical versions*, *view all history* — follow the part rights' rule and stand only where a viewing right is implied? ADR-0007 says they reach never beyond what the ladder reaches. | **They stand alone in the implied set.** A group granted a history right holds it whatever its viewing rights; the page that shows history applies the ladder's ceiling when it renders, showing only the versions the reader's viewing right reaches. | The ceiling is a property of what is shown, not of which rights are held: enforced where history is rendered, it needs no further inclusion rule and keeps the stored and implied sets simple. | `access/rights.py` unchanged; the history tab applies the ceiling | active |
| DL-0285 | 2026-09-28 | Who serves the platform's static files — the stylesheet and htmx — in production? The development server serves them only with debug on; gunicorn does not, and a host's own reverse proxy cannot be configured by the platform to serve them from the container. | **WhiteNoise, inside the application.** `whitenoise` joins the dependencies and its middleware the settings; `web` collects the static files as it starts, beside the migrations; the files are served under hashed names with far-future caching. | DL-0284 | 2026-09-28 | What must a password be? The first-run page is the first place the platform sets one, and the settings still carried the framework's default validators. | **Minimum 10 characters; at least one uppercase ASCII letter (A–Z), one lowercase ASCII letter (a–z), one digit (0–9) and one special character; only characters available on a standard US keyboard layout are allowed** — printable ASCII, a special character being any of it that is neither a letter nor a digit, the space included. **Two further checks:** the password is not on the common-password list, and it is not too similar to the person's own email address. The rule holds wherever a password is set: the first run, accepting an invitation, a reset. | The owner's choice. NIST SP 800-63B-4 advises against composition rules and sets 15 characters where a password is the only factor, as it is in release 0.1 (DL-0107); the common-password and similarity checks refuse the predictable passwords a composition rule invites. | `settings.py` (`AUTH_PASSWORD_VALIDATORS`, and a validator of the platform's own for the four kinds of character and the character set); the first-run, invitation and reset forms; the Ukrainian message catalogue (`django.po`) | active |
| DL-0283 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines DL-0281 for the platform's side of the proxy.) How does the platform name the proxies whose forwarded headers it trusts, when on one installation the proxy is the host's own at a fixed address and on another a container whose address Compose assigns? | **`TRUSTED_PROXIES` is a comma-separated list of IP addresses or CIDR networks.** A bare address is a one-host network; an empty value trusts no proxy, so the client address is the connection's and the forwarded headers are ignored. The key is parsed once at startup with the standard library's `ipaddress`, and a value that is neither an address nor a network is reported with the other configuration errors. Alternative: addresses only, networks added when production needs them (agent, declined — the parser is the same size either way, and production is already known to need a network). | A front container's address is assigned from a Compose network, so a list of addresses alone could not name it; a network names it whatever address it gets, and the same key serves the development host's proxy at its fixed address. | `.env.example`, `syngraphe/config.py`; the sign-in throttle and the middleware that read the client address, the scheme and the host from a trusted proxy's headers; `runbook.md § Deploy` | partly superseded — DL-0292 |
| DL-0282 | 2026-09-28 | (Completes DD-4's configuration rule for a process started outside the containers.) How does the test suite on a developer's machine, or a `manage.py` run on the host, receive the configuration that Compose injects into the containers from `.env`? | **The application loads `.env` from the repository root at settings import with `python-dotenv`, and a value already in the environment wins over the file.** The stack's own addresses — the database host and the two Redis URLs — are set on the `web` and `worker` services in `compose.yaml`, since the service names are the wiring; `.env` carries the host-side values a developer's process uses, and an operator running the stack leaves those keys at their defaults. Alternative: a loader private to the test suite, filling missing keys from `.env` in `conftest.py` (agent's first proposal, declined). | The standard Django shape: one loader for every process, in the library every Django developer expects, so the check line in `CLAUDE.md` runs on a developer's machine exactly as CI runs it. A loader only the suite knows about would be a second mechanism to discover. | `pyproject.toml` (`python-dotenv`), `syngraphe/config.py`, `compose.yaml` (the in-stack addresses on `web` and `worker`), `.env.example`, `deploy/compose.dev.yaml` (the database and Redis published on the loopback for host-side runs) | active |
| DL-0281 | 2026-09-28 | (Resolves DL-0117's deferral.) Where does production run, and what does the product carry for it? | **Production hosting is chosen: the platform runs behind a reverse proxy the operator owns, which terminates the public TLS and forwards to the platform over an internal TLS hop.** The product carries what any such installation needs and nothing about the installation itself: `deploy/` gains a production Compose override with a `front` service — nginx terminating the internal TLS with a certificate whose path is configuration, forwarding to `web`, which publishes no port there — and the header carrying the client's address is configuration too, since proxies differ. The base `compose.yaml` stays the development shape: `web` bound to the loopback port, the host's own proxy in front. ADR-0018 stands for this host: the archive and the bundles leave the host, and nothing about the database is kept on its disk beyond pgBackRest's spool. Where the installation runs, its proxy chain, ports, certificates, paths and backup store are the deployment architecture's, written at the first deploy and not published. | The override and the front's configuration are product files: a stranger deploying the platform behind their own proxy needs them, and a public file whose reason is private answers no reader. The estate they run in is nobody's business but the operator's. | `deploy/compose.prod.yaml`, `deploy/nginx.conf` (at the first deploy); `runbook.md § Deploy`; `technical-design.md § Runtime`; ADR-0018 (review trigger met) | active |
| DL-0280 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines ADR-0017.) How is a tenant created, what is it while its schema is not yet migrated, and is the step to active recorded? | **The tenant's row is written first**, in one transaction with its first administrator's membership and the creation's audit event, in state **`migrating`**; its schema is then created and migrated; `migrate_schemas` sets it to `active` when the migration completes, and a failed migration leaves it `migrating`, audited, with nothing dropped. **While `migrating`, the tenant is not resolvable**: no request reaches its schema. **The step from `migrating` to `active` is recorded in the tenant's state history**, like every other change of a tenant's state. Alternatives: the name `creating`, paralleling a document's creation (agent, declined); migrating first and dropping the schema on failure, writing no row (declined — it breaks *record before the fallible step*); recording creation and failure only as audit events, not the step to active (agent, declined). | Writing the row before the fallible step makes a half-made tenant visible and repairable rather than lost; the state history answers who changed a tenant's state and when, and activation is such a change. | `data-model.md` (`TENANT.state`, `TENANT_STATE_HISTORY`); `glossary.md` (*migrating*); ADR-0017; tenant creation's implementation | active |
| DL-0279 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines ASR-24 and ASR-25 of DL-0216.) A restore of the whole host may take up to four hours, while ASR-24 allows about three and a half hours of unplanned downtime a month. Which promise governs a disaster? | **Recovery from a disaster is counted apart from ASR-24's monthly measure, and ASR-25 governs it**: the loss of the host, its disks or its data, restored from the off-host copies, is held to ASR-25's recovery point and recovery time, not to the availability percentage. ASR-24 measures the platform's ordinary unplanned downtime — failures the running installation recovers from without a restore. Alternatives: lower ASR-25's recovery time to fit inside ASR-24's budget (agent, declined — one host without failover cannot promise it); leave the two inconsistent as an accepted risk (agent, declined). | A single disaster restore within ASR-25's four hours would by itself breach ASR-24 for its month, so the two requirements could not both hold as written; separating the disaster case keeps each measurable. | `asr.md` ASR-24 and ASR-25 | partly superseded — DL-0302 (which restores are counted apart) |

The epics and their stories are the release plan, kept outside this repository; DL-0125's rule that the spike epic runs before the rest are detailed stands. | The plan was rewritten on 2026-09-28 from the design closed that day; an active row saying *twelve epics* contradicts the epic names in `pages.md` and the verification plan. | DL-0125 marked partly superseded; V-14's epic reference in `verification-plan.md` | active |
| DL-0277 | 2026-09-28 | Who can read the off-host backup store, which holds every tenant's content? | **Only the platform operator.** Both backups are encrypted before they leave the host: pgBackRest's encryption for the database archive and base backups, `rclone crypt` for the repository bundles. The keys are per-installation values in `.env`, held by the operator and never written to the backup store; losing them loses the backups, so the runbook tells the operator to keep a copy away from the platform. Alternative: rely on the store's own protection, recorded as an accepted risk (agent, declined). | The store is off-host, often a third party's, and holds every organisation's documents together; without encryption, tenant isolation (ASR-01) and the threat model's confidentiality promise end at the host. | ADR-0018; `threat-model.md` — a threat for the backup store; `runbook.md` § Backup; `.env.example` when backup is implemented | active |
| DL-0276 | 2026-09-28 | (Supersedes, for a restore of the whole host, ASR-25's database recovery point in DL-0216; settles ADR-0018 before it is committed.) How do the document repositories meet ASR-25's fifteen minutes, and to which point is the database restored? | **Incremental bundles every fifteen minutes.** For each document repository whose refs moved since its last bundle, a worker task takes the writer lock, writes an incremental `git bundle` from the refs last bundled, verifies it and copies it to the backup store; the nightly full bundle after the base backup stays the base of each chain. **A restore of the whole host restores the database to the time the last complete set of repository bundles began**, so the repositories always hold every commit the database names (DL-0111): the recovery point of a full restore is at most fifteen minutes for both stores. **A database-only failure**, with the repositories intact on the host, restores the database to its latest archived point — at most five minutes. Alternatives: the database restored to its latest point with a reconciler reporting versions whose commit is missing (agent, declined); every commit pushed to an off-host mirror (agent, declined — the store types allowed have no git server). | As first drafted, ADR-0018 copied the repositories nightly, which could lose a day of commits; and a point-in-time restore past the bundle time would leave rows naming commits no copy holds — both found by a review before commit. Restoring the database no later than the repositories keeps the superset property the write protocol rests on; the price is up to ten more minutes of database changes in the rare loss of the whole host. | `asr.md` ASR-25; ADR-0018; `runbook.md` § Restore; `technical-design.md` § backup; the backup's implementation | partly superseded — DL-0296 (the bundle run's interval) |
| DL-0275 | 2026-09-28 | How is an exported file named? | **`<title>_—_<version>_—_<stance>.<ext>`** — the document's title as typed, the version number, the stance the export shows, joined by an em dash, with every space replaced by an underscore and the characters filesystems refuse replaced by a hyphen. Several files come as a zip of the same name holding `document.<ext>`, `appendix-<n>_—_<title>.<ext>` and `attachment-<n>_—_<original_name>`. The document's number is not in the name. The stance and the fixed words `document`, `appendix` and `attachment` are interface strings, written in the interface's language with the glossary's terms — in Ukrainian, `всі_неприйняті_правки`, `додаток-2`. **A marked export's name ends in a suffix naming the marks** — `<title>_—_<version>_—_<stance>-<marks>.<ext>`, the suffix written in the same language as the name's other fixed words (in Ukrainian, a synthetic example: `-правки`) — so a marked and a clean export of one version never share a name; a clean export has no suffix, and a zip of a marked export carries it too. | A file that leaves the platform is identified by what people call the document and by which version and stance it shows, and two exports of one version that differ only in their marks are still told apart in a folder; underscores keep the name one token in a shell and a mail client. The owner set the shape and the suffix (owner, 2026-09-28); the agent's proposal carried the number and kept spaces, and its draft let a marked and a clean export share a name. | `interface/pages.md § Export` (the file-names row), `technical-design.md § Export`, the export writer | partly superseded — DL-0502 |

A QA session or a measurement a person watches runs in a real browser and is not a test of the suite. | One dependency and one command for the bundle; the product needs no development server. Playwright runs under `pytest` beside the other levels, bundles its browser and gives the conformance suite's editor half a place in CI. Alternatives: Vite, which the spike used as a dev server; a browser level driven by hand, which would take the editor's conformance check out of CI. | `package.json`, `esbuild.config.mjs`, `deploy/Dockerfile`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (the bundle build and the browser install), `CLAUDE.md § Commands`, `docs/test-strategy.md §§ What CI runs, Levels` | active |
| DL-0273 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines DL-0112 and ASR-20.) The normalizer's *quote and dash typography* is named and nowhere defined. What does it do? | **In a line the edit touched, and nowhere else:** straight double quotes around a run of text become the document language's quotation marks — `«…»` for Ukrainian, `“…”` for English; a spaced hyphen between words becomes a spaced em dash; a doubled hyphen becomes an em dash; three dots become an ellipsis. Nothing else is rewritten. **The rules rewrite text content only**, after the splitter's masking — never a link destination, an attribute block, image syntax or a table's delimiter row — and run in the fourth and last of the normalizer's four steps, on what the three reuse steps left to rewrite and before the splitter places any boundary, over every touched line, a heading and a table row's cells included, though the splitter divides neither; the parse-preservation check therefore compares the typeset text before and after the split. Unicode composition to NFC, trailing whitespace and the canonical table row stand as before. | Policies are typeset documents and their exports carry the marks the language uses; a rule set this small is predictable, shows up as an ordinary change in *my edits*, and by ASR-20 never reaches a line the edit did not touch. Alternatives: quotation marks alone; no typography in the first release. | `technical-design.md § The constrained Markdown subset` (the normalizer's rules), the help page's allowed-Markdown list, the normalizer's tests | active |
| DL-0272 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines DL-0198 and DL-0267.) What number does pending work carry before a document's first acceptance? | **`0.0-X`.** The accepted number before any acceptance is `0.0`, so a new document's first version reads `0.0-1`; the first acceptance is `0.1`, as DL-0183 already numbers it. The `created` commit is not counted (DL-0268). **`0.0` names no version** — neither the `created` commit nor an edition, whose numbers start at `1.0` — and is never shown alone, linked or listed: it appears only as the base of `0.0-X`. Where a view marks pending work against the last acceptance and there is none yet, it marks against the `created` commit, the document's first text. | `N.M-X` needs an `N.M` to count from; `0.0` is the one value that makes the first acceptance's `0.1` follow without a special case, and it reads as *nothing accepted yet*. | `technical-design.md § Versions, holds and history`, `glossary.md` (*version number*, *edition*), the finishing screen's wording | partly superseded — DL-0358 |
| DL-0271 | 2026-09-28 | Which tools take the backups the write protocol requires — the database's continuous archive with point-in-time recovery, and a copy of every document repository that cannot catch a write half-done? The backup half is elaborated in `docs/adr/ADR-0018-backup-and-restore.md`; the Decision's last sentence, on a new tenant's document template, stands. | **pgBackRest for the database, `git bundle` for the document repositories, both into one off-host backup store.** pgBackRest archives the write-ahead log continuously and takes a nightly full backup; after each, every document repository is written as a bundle under its writer lock, verified, and copied to the same store. The database first, the repositories second. A new tenant's document template starts as one section, *General provisions* (*Загальні положення*), with no starting text. | pgBackRest gives an operator restore to a point in time, retention and verification as commands, which WAL-G leaves to scripts and plain `pg_basebackup` does not offer. A bundle is one file holding the whole repository that git itself proves complete and clones back exactly; a plain copy of a live repository can hold a ref to an object the copy missed. One section in the default template gives a new document a structure with nothing to delete. | `deploy/Dockerfile.db`, `deploy/pgbackrest.conf`, `compose.yaml`, `scripts/`, `runbook.md` §§ Backup and Restore; the tenant seed of `DOCUMENT_TEMPLATE` | partly superseded — promoted to ADR-0018 |
| DL-0270 | 2026-09-28 | Which dependency manager does the product use, and is schema-per-tenant routing a package or the platform's own code? | DL-0268 | 2026-09-28 | (Refines DL-0260 and DL-0267.) What kind is the first commit of a new or copied document, and does it count or notify? | **A kind of its own, `created`.** It is the first commit of a new document — the organisation's template — or of a copy — the chosen version's text. How long does the per-line authorship cache keep its rows? | **Rows are kept for two versions of each document: the head and the latest accepted version.** An older version's authorship — the historical page with *show author* on — is computed on demand by a worker job and kept for a limited time, never blocking: the text renders without an author until the job has run, as for any version without its rows. The alternative, a tag alone with the data model's sentence changed, was declined. The data model is brought in line with the prose: `VERSION.kind` gains `return`, `approval`, and the value that marks a content version committed by a holder of *accept* (ADR-0014), and loses `revert` — an undo, or a reverted part operation, is applied to the reviewer's draft and committed as the reviewer's version (ADR-0015), so no commit is a bare revert; `NOTIFICATION.event` gains `returned` and `correction_notice`, with a nullable key to the notice; `draft` leaves `DOCUMENT.state`, since `author_only` already carries the only fact it could mean; `VERSION.summary` starts from a mechanical draft in 0.1 and is agent-written only from 0.2. | The passport is the file the design names as what an auditor reads, and a ref cannot carry the approver; every other enumeration mismatch is a column Epic 3 or Epic 9 creates first, where an implementer would otherwise decide alone. | `data-model.md` (`VERSION`, `NOTIFICATION`, `DOCUMENT`, § Version numbers), `technical-design.md § Versions`, `document-lifecycle.md` | active |

| DL-0256 | 2026-09-27 | When a person who holds or has reserved a document is blocked, what happens to the hold? | **The block ends the hold.** A membership block (ST-0004), a self-block (ST-0066) and a tenant block (ST-0065) each end the person's holds and reservations on the documents they close to that person: the draft is preserved as after a forced release (ADR-0006), the release is an audit event recording the block as its cause and the draft's state as break-the-glass does (DL-0225), and the document is free. The alternative — the administrator releases the hold by hand as a second act, and the design says so — was declined by the owner. | A blocked person can no longer sign in to finish or discard, so a hold left behind would lock the document and its group links until an administrator noticed and released it separately; the block already expresses the decision that the person's access ends. | `docs/user-stories.md` (ST-0004, ST-0065, ST-0066), `docs/document-lifecycle.md § Who may do what`, `docs/data-model.md` (`HOLD.released_by`, `AUDIT_EVENT`), `docs/threat-model.md`; review finding F-26 | active |
| DL-0255 | 2026-09-27 | How do people edit a document together in the organisation the platform is built for, and does a reviewer ever not edit? | **One responsible author per document, who is also its reviewer**: he holds the document's vision and carries each iteration; his peers are editors who contribute minor changes — they shape his text and do not introduce whole sections, appendices or large changes. **Every reviewer is an editor**: while reviewing he changes what he does not agree with, so a reviewer who cannot edit is not a case the design serves. The owner sees the reviewer as the document's coach rather than as a software reviewer. **This is the happy path, expected for most documents, not a rule the platform enforces**: several substantial authors on one document, or a reviewer who is not its main author, remain possible and are served by the same rights and records. Whether *accept* therefore includes *edit* in ADR-0007's table, as it already includes the pending view and correcting (ADR-0001), is a separate question the review puts to the owner and is not decided by this row. | Facts the owner alone holds, asked by the review to rank its findings: they settle which correction-notice rule the review recommends (F-19 — a notice for every pending line replaced by a version made by a holder of *accept*, rather than for any line any peer changed) and let the accept-without-edit edge cases drop from the lifecycle table (F-24). | `docs/design-record/architecture-review-report-03.md` (F-19, F-24, the owner's question on *accept* and *edit*); ADR-0007 and `docs/interface/pages.md` only if the owner confirms the inclusion | active |

Who may lift it — a platform administrator as ADR-0007 says, or also a tenant administrator for their own organisation, which the owner raised and which needs a per-membership self-block — is decided when B-033 is taken up. Found by the staged review; the alternative, a story and a region carried under the next interface round, was not taken. | A self-block is rare and deliberate; the recovery path exists, and designing the control now would settle a question — who may lift it — that changes the access model. | `backlog.md` (B-033), `user-stories.md` (ST-0066), `docs/interface/pages.md` (users), `docs/interface/coverage.md` | active |
| DL-0253 | 2026-09-27 | (Extends DL-0232.) Does an image typed inside a sentence in the source view, or arriving that way by import, stand as it is? | **No — every image stands as a paragraph of its own, however it arrives.** A paragraph that holds an image and anything else — text, or a second image — is refused with a message naming its line and saying that an image stands on its own line; a paragraph whose only content is one image is accepted, in a list item or a callout too.
