| DL-0244 | 2026-09-27 | (Extends DL-0112.) How does the normalizer keep a moved paragraph's stored lines, so that authorship follows the text rather than the person who moved it? | **The normalizer reuses stored lines for unchanged text wherever it moves, in four steps, before anything is split.** (1) Each submitted paragraph's lines are joined and canonicalised. (2) **Whole paragraphs:** a paragraph equal to a stored paragraph anywhere in the file reuses that paragraph's lines byte for byte. (3) **Alignment:** any other paragraph is aligned against the stored paragraph at its place (DL-0112), which carries the stored lines across unchanged spans. (4) **Sentences:** in what is still unmatched, the previous version's stored lines — anywhere in the file — are the candidate sentences; one found as a whole-word run of the unmatched text, longest first and without overlap, reuses its bytes. Only what matches nothing is split and re-spelled. Where one text matches several identical stored lines, any choice leaves the same bytes; **which author it keeps is the authorship worker's to decide**, and the worker records an ambiguous case rather than guessing (ADR-0011). | Text arrives through the source view and outside pastes without any sentence boundaries, and the normalizer treats every path alike, so the bytes on disk are decided here and nowhere else; the stored lines are the only sentence boundaries that exist before a split, which is why they are the candidates. The normal form is path-dependent — a stored split may come from an older splitter or an earlier edit — so re-splitting a moved paragraph can change its bytes even when the text is unchanged. Authorship of moved text rests on verbatim lines (ADR-0011's exact-line rule, V-06); this lookup makes them verbatim on every path, and it is a hash lookup. Observing moves in the editor instead was weighed and rejected: a cut and a paste are separate transactions with no identity that survives copying, the source view or an outside paste. | `technical-design.md § The constrained Markdown subset` (moved text); the normalizer (Epic 4); V-06 | active |
| DL-0243 | 2026-09-27 | (Supersedes, for ST-0037, ADR-0003's *the platform resolving the case where a later author built on top* and the story's per-sentence choice.) How does undoing a contribution work? | **A reviewer's act, from the History tab, in two units:** *undo this edit* on any version after the last acceptance, and *undo all of this author's edits* for any author of the pending run. **The reversal is computed automatically against the latest commit and applied to the reviewer's draft** — the one they have open, or a new one that takes the document; nothing is deleted from the history, the removed text is taken out and the earlier text typed back as ordinary edits. **Sentences a later version changed are not reverted:** they are left as they are and listed at the top of the draft for the reviewer to edit by hand; choosing sentence by sentence or word by word is ordinary editing. Finishing commits it as the reviewer's version (*accept as* or *create version*), and every author whose text was removed receives a correction notice (ADR-0001). Editors no longer have the act — their own work is discarded with *discard my edits* — and accepted text is changed by editing. Owner's design; the agent pushed back that the per-version unit alone would drop the per-author promise V-11 proved today, and the owner chose both units (alternatives: per author only — agent's recommendation; per version only — owner's first description). | Automation earns its place where the reversal is mechanical; where a later author built on the text, the choice is an editorial one, and the editor the reviewer already has is the interface for it — a separate conflict screen would duplicate it. V-11's classification of untouched and built-on sentences is what separates the two cases. | `user-stories.md` (ST-0037), `document-lifecycle.md` (*revert a contribution*), `glossary.md` (*undo a contribution*); ADR-0003 and V-11 stay as written, read with this row; the scope agreement's bullet keeps its promise; `docs/interface/pages.md` (History tab) and `mockup.html` at the close of the interface session; `technical-design.md` (the single-writer lock, history) | superseded — promoted to ADR-0015 |
| DL-0242 | 2026-09-27 | (Supersedes the conversion clause of DL-0066; closes the contradiction the architecture review found.) DL-0066's other clauses stand: images are ordinary git blobs, no LFS, with an allowed-type list and a size cap. | DL-0074 decided refusal after DL-0066 decided conversion, and the technical design kept the conversion; the glossary and the user stories already follow DL-0074. Refusing needs no decoder, no quality decision and no conversion failure mode, for a format nobody needs to upload. | `technical-design.md § Rendering, styles and layout` (images); DL-0232 reads *type, size and metadata checks* | active |
| DL-0241 | 2026-09-27 | What does the second interface round settle? (editing and finishing) | **Export marks are measured against the version before in the chain**: a canonical edition against the previous edition (offered only with a history right), an accepted version against the current canonical edition, pending edits against the last acceptance, the holder's draft against the current text; the dialog names the baseline, and the default is clean for a canonical edition and marked for every other version (owner's default; baselines the agent's reading, confirmed). **The button keeps the glossary's name, *discard my edits***, in the edit tab and on the finishing screen alike (agent, accepted; alternative: the owner's *discard all my changes* everywhere). **A person's own reservations are listed under *My reservations* in the user menu**, each with its document and start date — the first content of the user menu beyond signing out and blocking one's account (owner; the agent had recommended a filter on *In edit*). ***Undo* and *redo* are toolbar buttons** in every editor as well as keys (agent, accepted). | Marks mean something only against a named baseline, and the chain's previous step is what a reader of that version would compare it with. | DL-0240 | 2026-09-27 | What happens to text that is inside the whitelist but spelled differently from the stored form — `*` bullets, `_italic_`, a setext heading, a table without edge pipes? | **It is accepted and rewritten to the one stored spelling; the rewriting rules belong to the normalizer.** The stored spelling: `-` bullets, `1.` ordered markers, a nested list indented by its parent's marker width, `*italic*` and `**bold**`, an entity span outermost when marks nest, `#` headings without closing hashes, pipe tables with edge pipes, a callout marker alone on its line, a literal `<` escaped, and so is a literal `&` written before a name that would read as an HTML entity. A line counts as touched only if it differs from the stored line after this rewriting (ASR-20). | The editor spike found that each of these spellings parses to the same document and is written back in one form, so a line stored any other way would change on its first save with no edit, and be re-attributed. | DL-0239 | 2026-09-27 | May a table stand inside a list item or a callout? | **No — a table stands at the top level of the document only**, never inside a list, a quote or a callout; such a table is refused with a message. | The table row is the unit of diff, authorship and per-author undo, and a canonical row has one spelling; an indented table inside another block gives every row a second, indented spelling and complicates both. | DL-0238 | 2026-09-27 | (Amends the whitelist of DL-0046 and DL-0100.) Are plain quotes allowed, and what happens to text written on a callout's marker line? | **A plain quote — `> text` — joins the whitelist** as a block of its own: paragraphs and lists inside it, never a nested quote, a callout or a table. **Text on a callout's marker line is accepted and moved into the callout's first paragraph**: `> [!note] Text` is stored as `> [!note]` on its own line followed by `> Text`. | DL-0234 | 2026-09-27 | (Extends ADR-0007.) A group may hold *make canonical* without the viewing right to read the accepted version it turns into an edition. What does *make canonical* include? | **Make canonical includes view accepted** — the text it approves — as *accept* includes view pending. | DL-0232 | 2026-09-27 | What happens to an image the user pastes or drops into the editor? | **An image already on the clipboard — a screenshot, a copied image's own bytes, an image embedded in copied text — becomes an uploaded image**: it passes the same type, size and metadata checks as any upload, is stored under `images/`, and the text holds only `![alt](images/name.ext)`. **It always becomes a paragraph of its own**, placed after the block holding the cursor — never inside a sentence or a table cell. **An image that exists only as a web address is not fetched**: it is dropped from the paste, the rest of the pasted text goes in, and a one-line notice says the image was not pasted and to save it and paste the file. | DL-0231 | 2026-09-27 | (Supersedes ST-0057's criterion *not a screen — a one-off operation at install time*.) DL-0210's aside that the first platform administrator is created from the command line now holds only for the token. | A page makes the first step of the walking skeleton visible and testable like every other, while proving access to the server exactly as the command did: the token is printed where only the operator sees it. | `user-stories.md` (ST-0057), `threat-model.md` (T-19); `docs/interface/pages.md` (sign-in pages) and `mockup.html` at the close of the interface session | active |

| DL-0226 | 2026-09-27 | (Extends ADR-0006's visibility rule for reservations.) The agent's alternative — ADR-0006's rule unchanged for reservations — was declined. | An ordinary hold asks colleagues to wait half an hour; a reservation lasts weeks and ends by agreement between people (DL-0214), so those it blocks — the people who could otherwise edit or review — need to know whom to talk to. | DL-0225 | 2026-09-27 | (Refines DL-0214.) What does breaking the glass on a reservation record and tell, and what can the holder compare the preserved draft against? | **The administrator gives a reason**; the holder receives a notification naming who ended the reservation, when and why; the audit event records actor, time, reason and the draft's state. On return the notice stands on the document: if nobody has committed since, the holder takes the hold back and may reserve again; otherwise the draft opens read-only as changes against the current text (ADR-0006). **The comparison defaults to the current text and may be switched to any version the holder may see.** The people concerned still settle it outside the platform, which is the default channel (DL-0214); the reason and the notice are its record, not a substitute. The agent's alternatives — no required reason, and comparison against the current text only with any-version comparison left to the backlog — were declined. | A reservation broken after weeks leaves the most work at risk of any hold; a stated reason and a notice make the break answerable, and comparison against an older version lets the holder see which of their changes were already overtaken. | `data-model.md` (`AUDIT_EVENT`, `NOTIFICATION`), `user-stories.md` (ST-0030), `technical-design.md`; the new ADR | active |

| DL-0223 | 2026-09-27 | (Refines DL-0218.) What does a reservation block while it lasts? | **Freezing and access changes, with no exceptions.** A reserved document cannot be frozen (a freeze needs nobody holding it, DL-0189), and its group links cannot change (ADR-0007); the administrator, who sees who reserved it, asks the holder outside the platform or breaks the glass (DL-0214). **A frozen document cannot be reserved:** the control is not shown during a freeze, though holders of *accept* and *edit* may still hold and edit it as today. The agent's alternatives — allowing link changes during a reservation when the holder keeps the right, and allowing reservations during a freeze — were declined. | Both existing rules protect the same thing, the one writer and the audience it writes for; exceptions would be new rules for a case break-the-glass already covers. | DL-0222 | 2026-09-27 | (Extends DL-0092.) When the holder also holds *accept*, can finishing an editing session produce the version and its acceptance in one act, and what does it accept? | **Yes — one act on the send-for-review screen, for a holder who also holds *accept*, commits the draft as a version and accepts the run** exactly as *send for review* followed by *accept the run* would, both attributed to that person; no new state and no new record type. **It accepts the whole run since the last acceptance**, other editors' pending versions included (ST-0036): the history is linear, so the holder has been editing on top of them, and accepting the current text accepts everything before it; correction notices go out as usual to every author whose text the holder changed (ADR-0001). **The choices name what each produces**, with the number the version will carry (DL-0198): a holder of *accept* sees *accept as 2.4* · *погодити як 2.4* and *create version 2.3-1* · *створити версію 2.3-1*, the numbers computed for the document at hand; a holder of *edit* alone keeps *send for review*. **The control that opens the screen reads *finish editing* · *завершити редагування* for everyone**, so nobody presses *send* to reach a screen without it; *send for review* as that control's label (agent) was declined. **The change summary of a version made by *accept as* shows in that version's history entry**, since it is never pending (refines DL-0197); dropping the summary there (agent) was declined. The owner found *send* meaningless when one sends to oneself, and *leave unaccepted* · *залишити неприйнятими* (agent) suggested the work would vanish; *put in review*, *add to pending edits* and *keep for review* (agent) were declined; *save* is a word the product never uses (`glossary.md`). **Deferred to release 0.2:** how the act relates to the reviewer agent's run, which starts at *send for review* — trigger: the design of the agent run. Keeping it one act over the existing records leaves the audit trail exactly as the two steps would. | `document-lifecycle.md` (editing session, *Who may do what*), `user-stories.md` (ST-0029, ST-0036), `glossary.md`, `prd.md`, `docs/interface/pages.md` and `docs/interface/mockup.html` (pending); 0.2 agent-run design | active |
| DL-0221 | 2026-09-27 | (Refines DL-0212.) In the data model it is a mark on `HOLD`, not a table or a state of its own; the document is *held* throughout, and others see *reserved for editing* instead of *being edited*. **Starting, cancelling and ending a reservation each write an audit event**, so the record shows every reservation a hold ever had; auditing only its end by an administrator (agent's alternative) was declined. The agent's alternative, a separate document state beside *Held*, was declined. | Reserving is done only from an editing session already open (DL-0219), so everything a hold guarantees must hold for a reservation too; a second state would duplicate those rules and let them drift. | `data-model.md` (`HOLD`), `document-lifecycle.md`, `technical-design.md`; the new ADR | active |

The agent's alternative — keeping the reservation across *send for review* so the holder could continue — was declined. | *Send for review* marks the iteration as ready for peers, which is the point the reservation existed to reach; carrying the reservation past it would keep the document from its reviewers' corrections. | `document-lifecycle.md`, `user-stories.md`; the new ADR | active |
| DL-0219 | 2026-09-27 | (Refines DL-0212.) How does a reservation start, and what happens to other people's preserved drafts? | **Only from an editing session the holder already has:** opening an editor takes the ordinary hold (ADR-0006), and the holder then reserves it. The agent's alternatives — also reserving from the document list without opening an editor, and reserving only from the start with no conversion either way — were declined. | One path in keeps the hold's rules whole: the reservation is taken by the same act that already establishes exclusivity, and nobody reserves a document they have not opened. ASR-23: documents up to 100 pages, body and appendices together; images up to 10 MB; up to 20 attachments within a 5 MB default cap; 50 signed-in users per tenant, 300 across the platform; tens of tenants on one host. ASR-24: 99.5% of each month, announced maintenance excluded. The holder's draft is preserved (ADR-0006) and, where the document has moved on, opens as changes against the current text. | The need is weeks of work under one hold, so any timeout would end it on the platform's clock rather than the people's. | DL-0209 | 2026-09-26 | Which version does a document's reading view show? | **The latest version the reader may see**: the latest pending version (`2.3-1`) for a holder of *view pending*, the latest accepted (`2.3`) for a holder of *view accepted*, the canonical (`2.0`) for everyone else. The document list's latest-version column and the document's header follow the same rule. **A document with no canonical edition yet answers a reader who may see only canonical text exactly as a missing one** — *Nothing to show here* — and does not appear in their lists until its first canonical edition exists. | An editor or reviewer opening a document wants the text as it now stands; a reader who may see only approved text must never meet work in progress. | DL-0208 | 2026-09-26 | What does the fourth round of layouts settle? | **The document list has two version columns**: the canonical version, linking to it, and the latest version, empty when there is none beyond the canonical one, linking where the title does. **The tab holding a document's metadata is named *Info*, at `/document/12/info/`.** **A historical page is marked by a banner** across the top, and its outline collapses like the document's. **Compare** opens a page of its own: links to the two versions compared, the newer version's whole text with the changes against the older marked in place; appendices are compared in pairs the reader picks, on a page of the same shape; attachments cannot be compared, so the page lists each with links to its file in both versions and says whether it changed. **The appendix page's title links back to its document.** **Export** stays unavailable while nothing is chosen; a part chosen that the chosen version does not have is reported as not available in that version, and a choice that leaves nothing to export says so. | Two columns answer the two questions a reader brings to a list — what is approved, and what is newest — without folding one into the other. | DL-0207 | 2026-09-26 | How do lists sort and link, and what do the document header, the appendix page and the export dialog show? | **Lists** sort by `#` or title, ascending or descending, title A to Z by default; in search results and in the *In edit* and *In review* views the sort applies within each folder group, the groups stay alphabetical, and only folders holding results appear. **The title opens the reading view; the pencil opens the edit tab.** A canonical version number in a list links to that version. **The document header shows the latest version only** — `2.3-1`, not the canonical `2.0` beside it; the reading view states which version it shows, with a link to the latest canonical one. **Search results are grouped under a heading per folder path.** **An appendix page offers no document actions** — no copy, freeze, make canonical, archive or groups. **In the export dialog an option that does not apply is disabled, not hidden**, and each version number offered links to that version in a new window.  | A layout that jumps as options are ticked loses the reader's place; a disabled option also shows what is possible. | DL-0203 | 2026-09-26 | How is every email the platform sends queued, retried and recorded? | **Every outgoing email is written to an outbox before any attempt to send it**, with sender, recipient, subject, text, time and status — *queued*, *sent*, *failed* — and the outbox is the log of every email the system sent. A tenant's mail is queued in that tenant's own schema; only platform mail sits in the shared one. A message is tried **up to three times, five minutes apart**; after the third failure it is *failed*, and the tenant's administrators and the platform administrators get an **in-application** notification of it — never an email, since mail is what failed. **With no sending credentials configured — neither the tenant's nor the platform's — no email is sent at all**; in-application notifications still work. **Sender settings** — the platform's, set only by a platform administrator, and a tenant's, set by that tenant's administrators or a platform administrator — are recorded in the audit log on every change: server, address, who and when; never the password, and not the port. | Recording before the fallible step means a crash or an outage loses no message and a failure is visible rather than silent. Retrying a few minutes apart rides out a short outage without flooding a server that is down. Warning about failed mail by mail would fail the same way. | `data-model.md` (EMAIL_OUTBOX, NOTIFICATION), `technical-design.md` (notifications), `user-stories.md` (ST-0048, ST-0049, ST-0068) | active |
| DL-0202 | 2026-09-26 | (Supersedes DL-0072's single sending address for every tenant; takes up B-009.) May a tenant send notifications from its own address, and how are sending credentials kept? | **A tenant may configure one sending address, or none**; with none, its notifications go from the platform's address. A tenant's administrator or a platform administrator enters the outgoing server, the address and its password. **Send a test email** sends, with the values on the form, a message from that address to that same address — subject *Test message*, text *Test email from {host}, sent at {time}* — and reports each step: connected, signed in, sent. Outgoing mail only; no incoming-server settings. **Every sending password — the platform's and each tenant's — is stored only encrypted**, with authenticated encryption under a key supplied by the environment and never kept in the database or the repository; the environment may hold the previous key beside the current one so the key can be rotated. The password is decrypted only in the worker at the moment of sending and never logged; the audit log records that it was replaced, never its value. In the interface it is **write-only**: it can be replaced, never shown. The administration screen states that reliable delivery also needs the tenant's own DNS records (SPF, DKIM). | Mail that carries the tenant's own name belongs with the tenant's own domain (ADR-0008). A test that only signs in proves less than a message that arrives, and sending it to the sending address itself needs no second address. A database dump without the environment's key yields no working sender (T-12). | `data-model.md`, `technical-design.md` (notifications), `threat-model.md` (T-12), `user-stories.md`, `backlog.md` (B-009, B-027) | active |
| DL-0201 | 2026-09-26 | How are a document's identity and addresses decided? | **Promoted to ADR-0008** — per-tenant document numbers, tenant resolution by domain or session, the URL structure, one not-found answer, `tenant-N/document-M/` on disk; from DL-0196, DL-0199, DL-0200. | See ADR-0008. | `docs/adr/ADR-0008-identity-and-addressing.md` (new) | superseded — promoted to ADR-0008 |
| DL-0200 | 2026-09-26 | (Supersedes DL-0111's naming of repositories.) How are tenant and document directories named on disk? | **`tenant-N/document-M/`** — `N` the tenant's number on the platform, `M` the document's number in its tenant (DL-0196), both assigned in order of creation and never reused. Replaces naming by internal identifier; the rest of DL-0111 — one repository per document, the write protocol — stands. | An operator reading the server should see which tenant and which document a directory holds without a lookup, and a repository restored without its row then names its own document. Numbers carry no title, so nothing confidential reaches filesystem listings or backup manifests. | `technical-design.md`, `data-model.md` (TENANT.number), `asr.md` (ASR-15) | superseded — promoted to ADR-0008 |
| DL-0199 | 2026-09-26 | What does a person see at the address of a document they may not open? | **Exactly what they would see at an address where no document exists** — one response for a document that does not exist, one they have no right to, one archived, and one in another tenant: the same status (not found), the same page, the same timing. The page says **"Nothing to show here"** — "The link may be incomplete or out of date. Check it, or ask whoever sent it." — with a way back to the document list. It never names a title, author, version or folder, and never mentions access. | Any difference between those cases — a message about rights, a different status, a slower answer — tells the reader the document exists, which is itself information they have no right to (ASR-16, ASR-17). | `technical-design.md`, `threat-model.md`, `docs/interface/pages.md` | superseded — promoted to ADR-0008 |
| DL-0198 | 2026-09-26 | (Supersedes DL-0183's notation for pending work.) How is pending work written? | **`N.M-X`** — the last accepted number, a hyphen, and the count of commits since it: `2.3-1`, `2.3-2`. Everywhere: the interface, exports, URLs (`/document/12/version/2.3-1/`). The rest of DL-0183 stands. | A hyphen needs no encoding in a URL, so version links stay readable; a `+` in a query string reads as a space. Clause numbers share the shape (`1.1-3`), but a section number and a version number already look alike, and each appears in its own place — clauses in the text's gutter, versions in headers and history. | `technical-design.md`, `glossary.md`, `user-stories.md`, `document-lifecycle.md`, `backlog.md`, `docs/interface/pages.md` | partly superseded — DL-0542 |
| DL-0197 | 2026-09-26 | How do the top menu, the filters and search present documents, and what does a history entry show? | **The menu carries no counts** and does not change with filters or search (supersedes DL-0194's counts). **All documents** browses by folder — a folder is always selected, the tenant's root included; there is no view of every document without folders (supersedes DL-0191 and DL-0194 on that point). **In edit** and **In review** are views of their own: the documents matching the view, with the folder pane showing only folders that contain them. **Search** looks in document titles and appendix titles only, never in text — in the latest version the searcher may see, so a title that exists only in pending work never matches for someone who may not see it; its results are listed grouped by full folder path, and the folder pane shows only folders with results. The page's content is at most 1,440 px wide, centred; the title column takes the remaining width. **A history entry shows the version, the event — edit, acceptance, return, freeze, canonisation — and the person who acted**; the editor's change summary stays the commit message and is **information for the reviewer**: it appears only with the version in pending edits, to those who may see them, and never in the history or an export. | Counts in a menu change under the reader and say little; a view says it all when opened. Always browsing inside a folder keeps the tree the organising frame, while filters and search cut across it and show where their results live. A history reads as who did what, and when. | `user-stories.md` (ST-0013, ST-0032), `docs/interface/pages.md`, `backlog.md` (B-026) | active |
| DL-0196 | 2026-09-26 | How is a document identified, how is a tenant recognised, and what are the application's URLs? | **A document is identified by a number per tenant** — `#7`, `#42`, `#1234`, no padding — assigned in order of creation, never edited, never reused; archived documents keep theirs and a copy gets a new one. It replaces the slug as the document's identity in the interface and in URLs, is shown before the title, and is the first column of the document list. **Appendices and attachments are numbered per document from 1** in order of creation; the number is their identity and their file name in the repository, and is independent of their display order. **Tenants:** a tenant may have its own domain, where it is recognised from the host and a person signing in lands in that tenant; the application is also served on its main domain, where the tenant is the one chosen in the session — there, a link opened while another tenant is chosen does not resolve to the linking tenant. Switching tenant moves to that tenant's domain, or stays on the main domain, and may need signing in again. **URLs:** `/document/12/` (reading view), `/document/12/edit/` and one segment per tab, `/document/12/version/2.3/` and `/document/12/version/2.3-1/`, `/document/12/compare/?from=2.0&to=2.3`, `/document/12/appendix/3/`, `/document/12/attachment/3/`, `/folder/7/`, `/search/?q=…`. | A short number is what people say and write when they refer to a document, and a URL built from it can be read aloud. Numbers restart per tenant, so the host — or on the main domain the session — says whose number it is. Numbering parts by creation keeps their links and file names stable however they are reordered. | `data-model.md` (DOCUMENT.number, TENANT_DOMAIN, part numbers), `technical-design.md` (tenancy, URLs), `asr.md` (ASR-15), `user-stories.md`, `docs/interface/pages.md` | superseded — promoted to ADR-0008 |
| DL-0195 | 2026-09-26 | What does the second round of layouts settle? | **Resizable side panes**: the outline (and the folder tree) and the right-hand panel collapse and can be widened or narrowed by dragging; each user's widths are kept in their preferences. **The right-hand panel's switchers are a row of icons along its top** — parts, generated views, comments — which also suits narrow screens. **Document tabs are plain underlined tabs**, without grouping labels. **My edits and pending edits show the whole document** with the changes marked in place, not the changed passages alone. **Every version opens as a historical page** in a new window: the reading view and export only, never editing, with *compare with* any other version, the latest included. **Help pages are Markdown files shipped with the application** and rendered by it; they are not documents on the platform and have no history there. **Every export is recorded** as an audit event. **A mobile layout follows** once the desktop layout is agreed. | Long titles need wider panes than any fixed width allows, and a width chosen once should stay chosen. A change read in its full context is a change a reviewer can judge. A historical version is evidence, so it can be read and exported but never edited. The help text describes the product itself, so it ships with the product rather than living as a tenant's document. | `docs/interface/pages.md`, `data-model.md` (user preferences), `technical-design.md` (help pages), `user-stories.md` (ST-0031, ST-0032, ST-0033) | partly superseded — DL-0536 |
| DL-0194 | 2026-09-26 | What does the top menu offer, and how are the document list and the document page arranged after the first round of layouts? | **Top menu, left:** *All documents*; *In edit* — documents the user may edit that have at least one version after their latest canonical edition; *In review* — documents with pending edits the user may accept; *Help* — how the platform works; *Administration*. *In edit* and *In review* are shown only to users holding the right on at least one document, show a count, and are disabled when empty; *Administration* is shown only to administrators. **Document list:** the title takes the space — long titles expected — with *being edited* written after it; type, version, part markers and an edit icon aligned right; appendices and attachments shown as present or absent, not counted; no export on the list; the folder tree has no heading, collapses with ▸ ▾, and its root is the organisation's name; administration actions in a ▾ menu. **Document page:** the outline shows every heading level, collapses with ▸ ▾, and lists no appendices; the right side is **one panel with switchers** — parts, generated views, comments — collapsible; the footer is gone and the document's metadata is an **Info** tab after *Analysis*; an appendix has a page of its own. Supersedes DL-0192's separate parts and comments columns and its outline entries for appendices. | Most documents are not being edited and most readers do not need every side view at once; the space goes to the title and the text, and the side views open when they are needed — comments most often. Presence of a part is the signal, not its count. The menu filters answer the two questions editors and reviewers ask first. | `docs/interface/pages.md`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0013) | partly superseded — DL-0542 |
| DL-0193 | 2026-09-26 | (Supersedes the composition half of DL-0172.) How is an export composed? | **One format per export**, chosen by radio button — DOCX, PDF or Markdown. **What** is chosen by checkboxes, all checked by default: *document*; *appendices*, either **embedded** in the document (the default) or as **separate files**; *attachments*, as their own files. A part the document does not have is not offered; the choice between embedded and separate appendices is enabled only when the document is included, and the format only when the document or appendices are — a choice that does not apply is disabled, never hidden (DL-0207); *Export* is unavailable while nothing is chosen, and a chosen part the chosen version lacks is reported, as is a choice that leaves nothing to export (DL-0208). **More than one file is always a zip**: the document in the chosen format, appendices as separate files if chosen, attachments as they are stored; the attachment table stays in the document. The view — canonical, accepted, pending edits, my edits marked or clean — follows the reader's rights (DL-0209). **No single-item export**: one attachment is downloaded from the document page, and one appendix is taken from the full set. A help button explains the choices. DL-0172's scopes, title page, attachment table and page furniture otherwise stand. | A person exports to use the result in one tool, so several formats at once serve nobody. Including attachment files lets a document leave the platform with the forms that belong to it. Single-appendix export is rare enough that taking it from the full set costs less than another control. | `technical-design.md § Export`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0045), `data-model.md` (EXPORT_JOB), `pages.md` | active |
| DL-0192 | 2026-09-25 | How is the document page laid out around its text? | **Four columns.** On the left, the **outline** — the body's sections, then each appendix. In the middle, the tabs. Then a **parts column** — appendices, attachments, and a section titled *generated views* holding the role directory and see also in 0.1, and the agents' views from 0.2. On the right, a **comments column**, wider than the parts column, for when comments arrive (B-013). **The outline and both right-hand columns collapse** to a strip and open again. Selecting an appendix shows it in the tab area under the one document header. Document actions — export, copy, freeze and unfreeze, make canonical, groups, archive, and force release in a *more* menu — sit in a bar under the title, each drawn only for those holding its right. Refines DL-0191's right column. | Comments need room of their own beside the text rather than a slot under the parts. Collapsing both lets a reader give the text the whole width when neither is needed. An appendix belongs to the document, so it is read inside the document's frame. | `docs/interface/pages.md` | partly superseded — DL-0542 |
| DL-0191 | 2026-09-25 | How are the document list and the document page laid out? | **The document list is the first page after sign-in**, and what is waiting for the user — notifications — sits in the header of every page: a table of the documents the user may see, with a title search above it (titles only, not full text), folder browsing or all documents without folders, the version state per row (the canonical number for everyone; accepted and pending — *2.0 · 2.3+1* — for those who may see them), icons for appendices and attachments, the title opening the reading view, and *edit*, *export* and *create document* where rights allow. **The document page:** tabs view · edit · my edits · pending edits · history · analysis; the **edit** tab shows the current text rendered, with an *edit* control beside each `#` section and *edit whole document*, and an opened section becomes an editor where it stands; **history** holds the version stepper, comparison of any two versions and the editions; the **right column** holds appendices and attachments together, with comments and generated views later. No companion-document icon in 0.1. | The list is where most users start and most work begins; the title is the fastest way into a document, and the actions a user may take belong on the row. Inline section editors keep the rest of the document in view while one part is edited. Appendices and attachments are the document's parts and sit together beside it. | `docs/interface/pages.md` (step 2), `user-stories.md` (ST-0013) | partly superseded — DL-0542 |
| DL-0190 | 2026-09-25 | ADR-0001, ADR-0002 and ADR-0003 are accepted and committed, and an accepted ADR is not edited. They predate parts of the ADR template. May their form be brought to it? | **Form only, once, and explicitly allowed.** Changed: frontmatter keys `aliases`, `supersedes`, `superseded_by`; the header block *Date · Status · Deciders · Supersedes*; the title separator `—` to `:`; the section heading *Options considered* / *Alternatives rejected* to *Alternatives Rejected*; ADR-0003's list of alternatives set as a table with an empty *Raised by* column, since who raised each option was not recorded; the italic origin line under each title moved, word for word, into a closing *References* section. **No decision, rationale, consequence or status changed, and nothing was added that the documents did not already say.** | A reader comparing ADRs should meet one layout. Moving existing text and renaming headings changes where things are, not what was decided; inventing the missing attributions would have changed the record. | `docs/adr/ADR-0001-*.md`, `ADR-0002-*.md`, `ADR-0003-*.md` | active |
| DL-0189 | 2026-09-25 | (Supersedes DL-0106.) What does freezing a document do, and how does a freeze end? | **A freeze locks the document against editors while something outside it is resolved** — an approval, a discussion with whoever approves (a board, for example), work in another document. The accepted version at the moment of freezing, and every version, stays exactly as committed. **A document can be frozen only when nobody holds it**; whoever wants to freeze it asks the holder, outside the platform, to finish, or waits for the inactivity release. **While frozen, only someone holding both *accept* and *edit* can edit**: they may work over several sessions, producing pending commits (`1.3+1`, `1.3+2`), and accept them as `1.4` when the document is ready for the next round; everyone who may see pending edits sees those commits as usual. **A freeze ends one of two ways**: *make canonical*, which makes the latest accepted version the next canonical edition (`2.0`) — refused while commits since that acceptance are unaccepted; or *unfreeze*, which returns the document to ordinary editing at its current accepted number (`1.3` stays `1.3`; the next commit is `1.3+1`). An **edition** is now a canonical version only; a freeze is a state of the document, not a version. | Letting everyone edit past a freeze means whatever returns to the approver carries what was added meanwhile, not only what was asked for. A freeze tied to one version would force a new freeze for every round of fixes; a freeze of the document lets the reviewer answer the approver in as many sessions as it takes. | `document-lifecycle.md`, `data-model.md` (FREEZE, EDITION), `technical-design.md`, `glossary.md`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0038 to ST-0040), `prd.md` | partly superseded — DL-0515 |
| DL-0188 | 2026-09-25 | How is access to documents granted? | **Promoted to ADR-0007** — rights held by groups, groups linked to documents, a person holds the union; new and copied documents author-only until the first group is linked; administrators read all content in this release. | See ADR-0007. | `docs/adr/ADR-0007-group-based-access.md` (new); data model, ASR-07, ASR-08, user stories, lifecycle, glossary, threat model to follow | superseded — promoted to ADR-0007 |
| DL-0187 | 2026-09-25 | Can a user compare two different documents, not only two versions of one? | **Yes.** From the document list or from a document, a user picks two documents and a version of each that their rights reach, and sees the same word-level comparison as between versions (DL-0170). The comparison does not know or care whether one document was copied from the other. **Appendices are compared only in pairs the user chooses** — one appendix from each document — because nothing tells the platform which appendix of one corresponds to which of the other. | Documents are copied (a copied document shares text but no history with its source) and organisations keep near-duplicates for different units; the question *how do these two differ* is the same question whatever their relation, and the diff answers it the same way. | `technical-design.md`, `user-stories.md`, `pages.md` | active |
| DL-0186 | 2026-09-25 | Is erasure of a person's name and address (ST-0056) in release 0.1? | **No — moved to backlog B-017**, the retention and erasure procedure, whose trigger is the first real document loaded. The tombstone shape of DL-0108 stands as the design the procedure will use; nothing in 0.1 removes it. There is no account deletion either — by the person or by an administrator (B-022); a person can block their own account. | The mechanism is designed and costs nothing to keep ready; the procedure around it — who may ask, who decides, what an export already made means — is operational and needed only once real people's data is held. | `user-stories.md` (ST-0056, Not in stage 1) | active |
| DL-0185 | 2026-09-25 | Which decisions of this design round are architecturally significant enough for an ADR? | **Three.** The document bundle — **promoted to ADR-0005** (from DL-0164, DL-0165, DL-0166, DL-0168, DL-0174, DL-0175, DL-0177). The editing surface — **promoted to ADR-0006** (from DL-0167 and DL-0180, with hold visibility and inactivity release decided with it). Group-based access and capabilities — ADR-0007, to be written once its open questions are answered. Version numbering, export composition, page furniture and attachment serving stay decision rows. | Each of the three changes the architecture or supersedes an earlier decision and weighed real alternatives; the rest are single rules with one row's worth of reasoning. | `docs/adr/ADR-0005-a-document-is-a-bundle.md`, `docs/adr/ADR-0006-editing-surface-over-one-draft.md` (new) | active |
| DL-0184 | 2026-09-25 | Do entity marks appear in the Markdown export? | **Yes, exactly as the source view shows them.** A Markdown export is the source view saved to a file: marks, headings and every construct as stored, paragraphs joined, frontmatter left out. Everywhere text is rendered — the reading view, the rich editor, DOCX and PDF — the syntax is hidden and **marked text carries a distinct formatting**, a shaded background for example, so a reader can tell an entity from ordinary text. | The source view already shows the marks to anyone who can export, so the Markdown export discloses nothing new; keeping them means text taken out and pasted back loses nothing. | `technical-design.md`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0026) | partly superseded — DL-0363 |
| DL-0183 | 2026-09-25 | How are versions numbered for the people who read them? | **Derived numbers, never typed labels, never commit hashes.** Each acceptance is `N.M`: N is the number of canonical editions before it, M counts acceptances since the last canonical edition and restarts at 1 — so the first acceptances are 0.1, 0.2. Making a frozen document canonical turns its latest accepted version into `N+1.0`; unfreezing changes no number (DL-0189). Pending work is shown as the last accepted number plus the count of commits since it — `1.3+2` — including in the header of a *pending edits* or *my edits* export. Numbers are recorded in the passport with each acceptance and approval. Freezing no longer asks for a label (ST-0038). | Users need to name a version aloud — in a meeting, in a letter, on a printed page — and a hash cannot be spoken. Tying the major number to canonical editions and the minor to acceptances makes the number say how far the text has moved since it was last approved. Deriving it removes the chance of two editions sharing a typed label. | `data-model.md` (ACCEPTANCE, EDITION), `technical-design.md`, `glossary.md`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0038, ST-0041) | partly superseded — DL-0198 |
| DL-0182 | 2026-09-25 | What page furniture do DOCX and PDF exports carry? | **Footer: the page number. Header: the document title with its version number beside it, and the stance the export shows** — canonical version, accepted edits, pending edits or my edits. Version numbers per DL-0183. | A printed page travels without its neighbours; the header is what tells its reader which document, which version and whether it is the approved text, and the page number is what lets a committee discuss it. | `technical-design.md § Export`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0045) | partly superseded — DL-0362 |
| DL-0181 | 2026-09-25 | What of the stored form does a user ever see — frontmatter, and one sentence per line? | **Neither.** The frontmatter is the platform's record of a document's parts and settings and is never shown or exported in any format. Sentences are stored one per line, and everywhere a user meets text — the reading view, the rich editor, the source view, and every export, Markdown included — they are joined back into paragraphs, one line per paragraph in Markdown. The normalizer restores the stored breaks when text comes back in. | Both are storage devices for the platform's benefit: the frontmatter makes a repository self-describing, the line breaks make attribution exact. A user who met either would have to learn a rule that serves no task of theirs, and a Markdown export that kept the breaks would fail the first time it was pasted elsewhere. | `technical-design.md`, `glossary.md`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0045, ST-0060) | active |
| DL-0180 | 2026-09-24 | How does an editing session end without being sent, and what happens to file operations when it does? | **The send-for-review screen offers three choices: continue editing, send for review, and discard my edits.** Discarding asks for confirmation, then throws the whole draft away — text, and every staged operation on appendices and attachments. A session that only uploaded, replaced, reordered or deleted attachments ends through the same screen with the same three choices; discarding it leaves every file as it was, so an attachment deleted and then discarded still exists. *Discard my edits* replaces *abandon* as the name of the act. | File operations are edits (DL-0168) and are staged in the draft (DL-0175), so they must be undoable in exactly the way text is; a deletion that took effect outside the draft would be the one change that skipped review. Putting discard beside send makes the way out visible at the moment of decision, and confirmation guards the one irreversible choice on the screen. | `prd.md`, `technical-design.md`, `document-lifecycle.md`, `glossary.md`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0022) | superseded — promoted to ADR-0006 |
| DL-0179 | 2026-09-24 | Attachments are arbitrary files handed from one user to another. What does the platform do about what they contain? | **Never render, never serve inline, never inspect.** Downloads pass the access check and are sent as downloads with a fixed generic content type and content sniffing disabled, under the stored file name; no public path serves them. Contents are not scanned in stage 1 — an accepted risk (T-15). ASR-04 now names attachments beside images; ASR-23's envelope counts appendices in the page budget and bounds attachments per document, within the unapproved proposal of DL-0151. | The damage an arbitrary file can do in this platform is either executing in a reader's browser, which serving it only as a download removes, or reaching a reader's machine, which inspection would only reduce and which the platform cannot rule out for a file it does not understand. | `threat-model.md` (T-15, accepted risks), `asr.md` (ASR-04, ASR-23) | active |
| DL-0178 | 2026-09-24 | Ukrainian wording sits in settled documents and in decision rows, some superseded. May they be edited to apply the English convention? | **Yes, once, for translation only.** This pass translated the Ukrainian in decision rows — superseded ones included — and in settled design-record documents into English, changing form and never sense. It is an explicit exception to the rule that a decision row is edited only within its own session and to the freeze on settled documents; it does not open them to any other kind of edit. The bilingual glossary is untouched by design. | A record read in two languages is a record half its readers cannot check. Translation preserves every decision's meaning, which is the thing the freeze protects. | `decision-log.md` (DL-0047, DL-0048, DL-0054, DL-0127), `architecture-review.md`, `markdown-vs-latex.md`, `backlog.md`, `technical-design.md`, `prd.md`, `document-lifecycle.md` | active |
| DL-0177 | 2026-09-24 | How are headings and clauses numbered inside an appendix? | **Each appendix numbers its own headings from 1** — `#` is 1, `##` is 1.1 — and its clauses the same way as the body's. A reference into an appendix carries the appendix in front: *Appendix 2, 1.1-3*. | An appendix is read and cited as a unit of its own; continuing the body's numbering would renumber every appendix whenever the body gains a section. | `technical-design.md`, `prd.md`, `user-stories.md` (ST-0023) | superseded — promoted to ADR-0005 |
| DL-0176 | 2026-09-24 | (Supersedes the language half of DL-0047.) Which language names the product's concepts, and where do translations live? | **English is canonical** for every concept, the stance names included: *Canonical version · Accepted edits · Pending edits · My edits · My edits in review*. **Every translation lives in `glossary.md`**, which is bilingual by design; no other document carries Ukrainian interface names **unless its frontmatter declares more than one language** (`language: [en, uk]`), which marks a document bilingual by design. Vocabulary settled with it, each term's Ukrainian name fixed in the glossary: *canonical version*; the accepted stance is headed *accepted edits*; the reviewer's act is *accept edits*, rendered with the Ukrainian verb for agreeing a document; the numbered unit is a **clause**, one paragraph of text; *folder* and *generated view* keep their Ukrainian names. | DL-0172 | 2026-09-24 | What can a user export, and in what shape? | **Export is composed with checkboxes**, never picked from a list; with nothing checked, nothing exports, and choices a document cannot offer are not shown. **Scopes:** the document alone; the document with all its appendices embedded; the document and its appendices as separate files in one archive; one appendix; one attachment (as the file itself); one companion document — in 0.1 a mechanical analysis or a change summary, from 0.2 an AI note. **Comments are not exported in 0.1.** Appendices are all-or-none except when exported one at a time. **Formats:** DOCX, PDF and Markdown for every text scope. **Views:** the canonical version; *pending edits*, with changes shown against the last approved text; *my edits*, with changes shown against the current text; *my edits* without marks. | DL-0168 | 2026-09-24 | How do images and attachments appear in the review of a run? | **An operation on an attachment is an edit of the document**, even when no character of the text changed: adding, replacing and deleting one each produce a change in the run. The reviewer sees every attachment added, replaced or deleted, with its title, can download the versions, and **can revert the operation** as part of correcting before accepting (ADR-0001). An edition records which version of each attachment it contains. **Images in the text are reviewed the same way**: the reviewer sees that an image was added, replaced or deleted and can view each version; there is no visual diff of images, and neither the companion nor the reviewer agent reads them. | ASR-04 requires that nothing enters the approved state unseen. | DL-0167 | 2026-09-24 | (Resolves DL-0159.) Does a user edit the whole document or one section at a time? | **Both.** The whole-document editor is always available, and every `#` section offers its own editor; **several section editors may be open on one page at once**. Section editing is **locality, not concurrency**: opening any editor takes the hold on the whole document (ADR-0003 stands unchanged), every editor writes into the **one draft**, and a section is never sent, reviewed or accepted on its own. **The unit of an edit is the document** — its body and appendix files — never a section. Only when every open editor is closed has the draft landed, and only then can it be sent for review or analysed. **Conflicts between editors are resolved per range.** Each editor — a section editor, the whole-document editor, or either in a second browser tab — remembers the text of the range it opened; the server accepts its capture only while that range of the draft still matches what the editor last saw. Editors over disjoint ranges never conflict. An editor whose range changed underneath it stops, becomes read-only, and shows the user's unsaved text as word-level changes against the current draft, to be carried over by hand. The server tracks open editors by heartbeat; sending for review or requesting an analysis is refused while any is open, and an editor whose tab closed without closing it times out. Typing a new `#` heading inside a section editor does not split that editor; once it is closed and the text rendered again, each new section offers its own editor. **The whole-document editor cannot be opened while any section editor is open** — the user is asked to close them first. Appendices offer the same editors. **DL-0126's removal of section files is not reversed**: a section is a range of a file, never a file. | Correcting one paragraph of a twenty-page policy in an editor that shows only that part lowers the load and the risk of deleting something unrelated, while restructuring across sections needs the whole document; offering one without the other fails one of the two tasks. Keeping the hold and the draft per document means nothing in the linear model changes: a section editor is the same editor over a range. | ADR-0003 (none — confirmed), `technical-design.md` (editor), `pages.md`, V-01 requirements, Epic 4 | superseded — promoted to ADR-0006 |
| DL-0166 | 2026-09-24 | (Resolves DL-0158.) What is an attachment? | **An opaque file the platform stores and never interprets.** A user adds one, replaces it with an updated version, deletes it, and downloads it; it cannot be edited inside the application. Every attachment has a **mandatory title** and no other descriptive field. Replacing one keeps its earlier versions. Attachments are listed in the order they were added and can be reordered by hand. **Size limit 5 MB per file by default, configurable by the tenant administrator**; at that size they are ordinary blobs in the document's repository, so one history and one restore path cover them. The AI half ignores attachments entirely. In release 0.1. How they are reviewed: DL-0168; how they are exported: DL-0172. | Documents in this domain ship with forms and templates that people fill in elsewhere; the platform's job is to keep the right version beside the text, not to understand it. | DL-0165 | 2026-09-24 | (Resolves the appendix half of DL-0157.) What is an appendix, and how does it live beside the body? | **An appendix is text of the same kind as the body and part of the same document.** Same constrained Markdown, same editor, same review cycle; a run, an acceptance and an edition cover the body **and** its appendices together. A document has zero or more appendices; a user adds and deletes them. Each has a **title**, entered in a field when it is created or edited; the user may write any number into it (*Appendix 1 …*), and nothing is derived from position. **That title is not the appendix's heading**: an appendix file holds **exactly one `#` section**, carrying the heading rendered in the finished document, and the entity title is never rendered in DOCX or PDF — it names the exported file, normalised. Appendices are listed **in the order they were added, and a user may reorder them by hand**. **An appendix is a leaf**: it has no appendices and no attachments of its own — attachments belong to the document. In the repository each is **a file named by an internal identifier, never by its title**. **This supersedes the one-file half of DL-0126**: a document is `document.md` plus its appendix files in the same repository; DL-0126's removal of *section* files stands. In release 0.1. | An organisation's appendix is approved with the document it belongs to and changes with it, so a separate lifecycle would let a body and its appendix be approved against different versions of each other. One repository keeps one linear history (ADR-0003) and one diff for the reviewer. Naming files by identifier keeps titles out of filesystem paths for the same reason repositories are named by identifier, and lets a title change without a rename. | `data-model.md`, `technical-design.md` (repository layout, restore from files alone, render chain), `document-lifecycle.md`, Epics 3, 4, 5, 7 | superseded — promoted to ADR-0005 |
| DL-0164 | 2026-09-24 | (Resolves the title-page half of DL-0157.) | DL-0112 | 2026-09-21 | (Closes review finding F-08; revises DL-0110 after the design consultation's second round.) How is a stored line kept from being re-normalised? | **No normalizer rule is ever applied to a line its edit did not touch** — the splitter and every other rule alike. *Touched* means: the submitted line differs from the stored line **after canonicalising the submitted one**, so a prettifier that pads a table row produces rows that canonicalise back to the stored bytes and count as untouched. Because the editor returns paragraph text rather than lines, *untouched* is **recovered by alignment**: the normalizer is a function of the previous version and the submitted text, carrying old boundaries across unchanged spans and splitting only changed spans widened to the whole old lines they overlap. The **hand merge and split is dropped**; instead an invariant checked on every write — **normalisation preserves the parse**. The normalizer version is recorded **per commit**, not per document. | The reviewer's three corrections, each one right. The editor never hands back lines, so the rule needs an alignment step I had not named. A wrong boundary degrades granularity, not correctness — an over-split renders identically and anchors identically — so the user-facing control contradicted DL-0012 for no gain; the only way a boundary can be *wrong* is a break inside an inline construct, which the parse invariant catches. And one document holds lines split by several versions at once, so a per-document version is untrue on the day of the first upgrade. | `asr.md`, `technical-design.md` | active |
| DL-0111 | 2026-09-21 | (Closes review finding F-03; answers question A of the design consultation.) What is the write protocol between git and PostgreSQL, and what is a repository? | **One repository per document**, named by `DOCUMENT.id` rather than by slug. **Git is the commit point** — the ref update is the moment a version, an acceptance or a return exists; rows are derived idempotently by the request and again by a reconciler that replays any commit lacking its row. A **single-writer lock per document** covers every ref update. **The passport wins** over the row, and the reconciler reports disagreement as an audit event. **Document creation is the one two-store write**: the row is written `creating`, the repository is initialised and receives the manifest commit, the row becomes `draft`; the reconciler retries a row without a repository and reports a repository without a row as an orphan, never deleting it. **Backup** takes the database first and the repositories second — the superset property holds per document because history is append-only and there is no cross-document commit — with each repository copied under its own writer lock or by a filesystem snapshot, and the database under continuous archiving, because holds, access rows and above all **drafts** exist nowhere else. A repository restored without its row is recreated with **no access rows at all** and the tenant administrator notified. | The owner's argument settles the granularity: a document *set* is not a stable grouping, because every reviewer assembles a different review scope. The reviewer added the argument that decides it — with one repository per document, **the agent's mount list is the review scope**, so the editor-facing run simply does not have the confidential guidance on its filesystem. That turns DL-0095's containment from a promise about prompt assembly into a mount table. Recorded losses: there is no commit meaning "the corpus as of this moment", which nothing needs; and a cross-document generated view has no repository to live in and will need a per-tenant derived store when B-003 is taken up. | `technical-design.md`, `data-model.md`, `asr.md`, `threat-model.md`, `backlog.md` | partly superseded — DL-0200, DL-0455 |
| DL-0110 | 2026-09-21 | (Closes review finding F-08.) How are sentence boundaries kept stable? | Superseded by DL-0112. **A boundary is decided once and stored, never re-derived.** The newline in the file is the boundary; the normalizer runs the splitter **only over lines the edit touched**, leaving untouched lines byte-identical, so improving the splitter later affects new text only. The splitter is rule-based per `LANGUAGE` with an abbreviation list, deterministic, and its version is recorded in the document manifest for diagnosis. An editor may merge or split a line deliberately, which is the escape hatch when the splitter guesses wrong on an abbreviation. **No special delimiter character.** | The failure the review named is re-splitting: a later, better splitter re-splits existing text and every re-split line is re-attributed to whoever commits next. Versioning the splitter records the damage; not re-splitting prevents it. The owner's instinct — make the boundary explicit rather than guessed — is the right one and the newline already is that explicit mark; a rare character would pollute the source, need stripping on every render and export, and cost the plain-readable Markdown that was half the reason for choosing it. | `asr.md`, `technical-design.md` | superseded — DL-0112 |
| DL-0109 | 2026-09-21 | (Revises DL-0086.) A small first tenant and no agents in stage 1, so there is no load to spread and no runtime to isolate. A VDS is provisioned when the platform leaves the lab. | Owner directive. DL-0086's reasoning for separate hosting was PDF export load and agent isolation; the first is not a problem at this scale, and the second does not exist until stage 2. | `technical-design.md` | superseded — DL-0117 |
| DL-0108 | 2026-09-21 | (Closes review findings F-06 and F-09.) Where does divergence re-enter, and what does erasure do? | **F-06:** ADR-0003 states that divergence is confined to two places — per-author undo, and a draft preserved past a forced release. A **stale draft opens read-only beside the current text** for the editor to copy from; it is never replayed onto moved text. The blame-based undo is **spiked before the scope agreement keeps promising it**. **F-09:** erasure **tombstones** a user — display name and address removed, identifier and a *former user* label kept — so the audit trail stays whole and ASR-18 keeps its answers. | Both were claims the set made and could not keep. ADR-0003 claimed no conflict interface while two stage 1 features are one; the data model claimed erasure removes a person from the record, which would empty the audit answers the platform sells. | DL-0104 | 2026-09-21 | (Owner ruling relayed from the review, DL-0099.) | DL-0093 | 2026-09-21 | (Refines DL-0088.) What recovers the readability that removing personal data from commits cost? | Two changes. | DL-0092 | 2026-09-21 | (Resolves: Q36.) May a reviewer accept their own edits, or approve an edition they froze? | Yes to both. | DL-0084 | 2026-09-21 | What happens when a document is archived? | Archiving and unarchiving are visible to **tenant and platform administrators only**. | DL-0082 | 2026-09-21 | How is every change attributed and reconstructible? | Two mechanisms, deliberately not one. **Row provenance** on every table: `created_at`, `created_by` and, where rows mutate, `updated_at` and `updated_by`, with an `actor_kind` of `user`, `system` or `agent`. **Typed append-only history** for the authorisation-bearing tables — `MEMBERSHIP` and `DOCUMENT_ACCESS` — so the question *"who could read this document on the day that edition was approved"* is answerable by query rather than by inference. Everything else attributable goes to a generic `AUDIT_EVENT` row: actor, action, object, before, after, timestamp. | Owner requirement. One generic log alone cannot answer the temporal authorisation question without replaying every event; typed history for every table alone would be a dozen near-identical tables nobody reads. Splitting by whether the answer must be *reconstructible at a past date* is the discriminator. `actor_kind` exists because this platform has three kinds of actor and an agent-written row attributed to a person would be a lie in the audit trail. | `data-model.md`, `asr.md` | active |
| DL-0081 | 2026-09-21 | How is agent-workflow detail kept from expanding indefinitely? | Detail beyond stage 1 stops being designed and goes to `backlog.md` as a question for stage 2. | DL-0079 | 2026-09-21 | What is the access model, in full? | **Three application roles.** *Platform administrator* — grants platform administration, edits users and access rights. *Tenant administrator* — grants access within their tenant, creates users by email, blocks users within their tenant only, assigns side-document types and companion actions, creates documents. *User* — edits and creates documents. The list is indicative, not exhaustive: ordinary Django surfaces such as editing one's own profile are not enumerated and are not thereby forbidden. **Four rights per file** — read, edit, accept edits, approve canonical — to which the agent adds a fifth, **comment**, because a reviewer without edit rights and a stakeholder who may not edit both need it and neither is served by *read*. **Cross-tenant:** no linking and no analysis across tenants; a user with several memberships switches tenant, and a user with one sees the tenant name and no switcher. | Owner directive. | DL-0069 | 2026-09-21 | (Resolves the storage half of the analysis question.)
